Kirlian Photography: Building Your Own Equipment
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-08
04:14:12 UTC
13:14:12 KST

Kirlian Photography: An Introduction
What is Kirlian Photography? Kirlian photography is a high voltage, contact print photography.
Kirlian photography is named after Semyon Davidovich Kirlian and his wife Valentina who began their work with high voltage photography in 1939. Kirlian collaborated with his wife for over 30 years developing equipment and studying electro-photography.
Kirlian's work was first made known to the general public in this country by a book published in 1970 by Shelia Ostrander and Lynn Schroeder titled "Psychic discoveries Behind the Iron Curtain". Kirlian work became so well know that the field of high voltage electro-photography is called Kirlian Photography.
Although Kirlian wasn't the first to study electro-photography. The field of electro-photography can be traced back to the late 1700s. At this time Georg Christoph Lictenberg noted the pictures made in dust create by static electricity and electric sparks. Nicola Tesla (1880) photographed many corona discharges using his famous Tesla coil. In the early 1900s, Russian researcher Yakov Narkevich-Todka exhibited electro photographs he had made. A little later, Dr. F.F. Strong of Tufts University Medical School used a Tesla coil to make electro-photographs of his hand.
Making Kirlian Photographs
The process is simple. Sheet film is placed on top of a metal plate, called the discharge or film plate. The object to photograph is placed on top of the film. If the object to be Kirlian photographed is inanimate, such as a coin or leaf, a earth ground is connected to the object. See figure 1 below. High voltage is applied to the plate momentarily to make an exposure. The corona discharge between the object and discharge plate passes through and is recorded onto the film. When the film is developed you have a Kirlian photograph of the object.
The Kirlian process, being a contact print process, doesn't require the use of a camera or lens. However when a transparent electrode is substituted for the discharge plate it is possible to use a standard camera (with a bulb setting) or video camera.
Figure 1
Paranormal Claims
I was first introduced to Kirlian photography, by the book titled "Psychic Discoveries Behind the Iron Curtain" by Shelia Ostrander and Lynn Schroeder in the early 1970's. In it many paranormal claims were made concerning the resulting Kirlian images. For instances, it was said that the Kirlian photograph could foretell illness in plants and animals before physical manifestations of the disease became apparent. I have experimented with Kirlian photography over the years. I have not observed any "paranormal" evidence of this phenomena.
One must keep in mind that most observable Kirlian phenomena does not require any paranormal or bio-plasma field to be explained. For instance, stress or the "act of lying" can easily be detected with a lie detector that relies on measuring the change in a person's galvanic skin resistance. Stress may also be seen in a Kirlian photograph as a change in the corona discharge (aura), however this change is easily explained by the change in a person's skin resistance without the need of evoking any metaphysical properties.
The Phantom Leaf Experiment
The one observed phenomena and, the most amazing claim (in my opinion) is known as the "phantom leaf" experiment. Here a small portion of a leaf is cut off; the leaf is then photographed using Kirlian photography. In a small percentage of cases the cut portion of the leaf appears in the photograph as a ghostly apparition. The appearance of the cut portion of the leaf, as claimed by the Soviet researchers is proof of an ethereal bio-plasma body.
Although a few Kirlian researchers have claimed to duplicate the phantom leaf experiment in their own labs, the most reported successful results (phantom leaf photographs) are from Soviet researchers. The exact experimental parameters (voltage, frequency, etc) needed to obtain phantom leafs are either not available or didn't work for me.
Kirlian Photography Devices for Sale on the Internet
There are Kirlian devices being sold on the internet today. To sell their devices the corona discharge in the resulting kirlian photograph is claimed to be paranormal or hold information that can be decrypted by the company's Kirlian researchers. All my research to date shows the corona discharge can be explained by employing known physical laws, like changes in subjects skin resistance (due to factors like; stress-lying, illness, fatigue, alcohol consumption, etc) without the necessity of invoking paranormal (bio-plasma) factors. Some other variable factors influencing the resulting Kirlian picture including the object's pressure against film, air humidity, air pressure, voltage, frequency, and exposure time.
This company also sells Kirlian devices and components, but we do not make any "paranormal" claims to try to sell our devices. Our devices are sold to make brilliant Kirlian photographs. If one wants to do research with our devices that fine also. Just know we make no claim that any interpretation of the corona discharge is due to anything other than standard variable physical parameters as outlined previously.
Building a basic manual Kirlian device
The next page we will begin the construction of a simple manual kirlian device. This device uses a battery power supply, high voltage ignition coild, copper clad board and a handful of components.
Building a Simple Kirlian Photography Device
The Kirlian device we are building uses a HV transformer. It is battery powered, but don't let that lull you into a false sense of safety. The Kirlian device generates pulses of high voltage that can provide a nasty shock. Still an advantage of being battery powered is portability.
In addition we will build a transparent electrode that allows one to use a standard camera (with bulb setting) to capture Kirlian images.
The schematic is shown in Figure 2. A completed circuit can be seen below the schematic. This is a simple manual device. It uses very few components. You would be hard pressed to find a simpler device that works as well. The prototype is built on a single piece of wood measuring *" x 10" x �" thick (see photo). The batteries B1 and B2 are 67.5-volt photo (instrument) batteries wired in series to produce 135 volts.
Figure 2
If the photo batteries are not available you may want to try wiring 10 or more 9-volt batteries in series. The discharge will not be as intense, but it will work. Another option is to take the output from a 120V/24V step down transformer connected to a voltage quadrupler. With the 24V (AC) output wired to a voltage multiplier (4x), the unit should produce about 100 volts that can be used for the power supply for this Kirlian device.
The capacitors C1-C4 are wired in parallel as shown in Figure 2.
The transformer T1 is a high voltage auto-transformer. Auto as in self-inductive, not auto, like automobile. The T1 transformer has three wires coming out of it: two enamel wires and one green insulated wire from the center. The green insulated wire is the high voltage wire that connects to the discharge plate. The two enamel wires are the primary wires that are connected to switch SW1. There is no polarity with the enamel wires. So it doesn't matter which enamel wire is connected to the switch, and which is connected to the capacitors. Either way the circuit will function properly.
Figure 3
The toggle switch SW1 is a single pole double throw "momentary" contact. The switch has solder wires on the bottom, see Figure 3. This particular switch doesn't lock or stay in the opposite position. A built in spring immediately brings the switch back to its original position. To operate the device, toggle the switch on and off. When the switch is in the "on" position, it closes a current path to the HV transformer and capacitor bank.
The switch is important and must be wired correctly to obtain maximum benefit. Looking at the schematic shows how the switch appears in its resting state. In the resting state the batteries are disconnected from the circuit. This preserves the life of the batteries. Also the discharge path from the capacitors is closed. This prevents a potential shock hazard from being stored in the capacitors, to be unleashed on an unsuspecting experimenter.
To mount the switch to the board a metal battery clip (9-volt) is used, see parts list. The clip is bent to make a U shape as shown in Figure 3. The center hole in the clip is enlarged to fit the switch SW1. A hole is drilled in each ear of the clip to secure it to the board with two wood screws. The switch is secured to the clip, and then the assembly is secured to the board.
Below is an example of a photograph taken with the Kirlian device.
Exposure Plate
The exposure (or discharge) plate is made from 4" x 6" single sided copper clad board. The high voltage output wire (green) from transformer T1 is soldered to the corner of the copper clad board. The copper side of the board is placed face up and glued to the wood board.
After the glue dries, the discharge plate is covered with a thin plastic sheet approximately .005 inch thick. The plastic should extend at least 1" past the discharge plate on all sides. The sheet is secured in place by taping or gluing it down.
The thickness of the plastic sheet isn't critical. Anything around this thickness that will prevent the object from making direct contact with the discharge plate. Sheets are available from many art and drafting supply stores. The plastic sheet may be transparent or opaque, it doesn't matter.
To test the circuit, attach a wire to point A shown on the schematic and place the other wire about ¼" away from the discharge plate or the uninsulated end of the high voltage wire. Note the discharge plate should not have the plastic sheet covering it during the test. Every time you flick the switch SW1 you should see a spark jump from the HV wire to the discharge plate.
When the circuit checks out properly, mount the components on the board using glue or epoxy.
Coat any exposed wires with a plastic spray to provide insulation (No-Arc spray is available at your local Radio-Shack stores.)
Cover discharge plate with plastic sheet.
Sheet film used for Kirlian Photography
Black & White Film Kodalith 2556 Ortho film type 3 is a high contrast B/W graphics arts film available in a 4" x 5" sheets. This film is perfect for beginners because you can use a red safelight and not work in complete darkness. If a safelight isn't readily available you can try using a red LED, neon lamp or wrap red acetate plastic over a dim 4 watt bulb.
I advise all beginners to start with black and white ortho film. It is less costly and easier to work with than color sheet film. You also have the opportunity to develop the film to get immediate feedback. Someone who starts off shooting color film must wait to get the film develop to see what has been recorded.
Developing B/W ortho film is easy. The chemistry works quickly and is simple and forgiving. You only need two chemicals; developer and fixer. A stop bath is usually employed in between these two steps, but isn't absolutely necessary.
Black and White (film or prints) is not as spectacular as color. The experience one gains by being able to observe all the steps under a safelight is invaluable when one has to work in complete darkness with color film. Using a high voltage power supply in the dark can be a pretty daunting task, so it's best to familiarize yourself with the process in the least costly manner.
Color Film – Daylight or Tungsten Balance Color film requires exposures to be made in total darkness. Working in total darkness can be a problem. Sometimes I sandwich the color film between two black opaque sheets of paper in total darkness, then turn on the safelight. After I make my exposure, I turn off the safelight and place the film in a light tight box for development in total darkness.
Both tungsten balance and daylight film give striking color transparencies. The tungsten balance film usually produces colors in the yellows, oranges and reds. Daylight film usually produces colors in the blues and greens.
Making Exposures Using Sheet Film
What you are photographing determines whether or not the object involved should be grounded.
Whenever you photograph a living subject (person, animal or pet) under no circumstances should that subject be grounded or be allowed to touch a ground during exposure. Being in contact with a ground will lead to a nasty shock. Further, anyone with a heart condition or pacemaker should consult with their primary care physician before photographing or allowing themselves to be photographed using this Kirlian device.
When photographing an inanimate object such as a coin, keys, leaf, etc. then connect a ground to the object. Grounding the object produces a stronger corona discharge. You can use a natural ground by connecting a wire to an earth ground, such as a cold water pipe. Or you can use a circuit ground by connecting a wire to point B (see Figure 2) to the object.
Figure 4
Figure 4 illustrates the overview for making an exposure. In some cases you might place a flat sheet of glass (or plastic) on top of the object to make the object lay flat on the film and discharge plate.
Whether you're working with black and white or color film, place the film emulsion side up on the discharge plate. Place the object you are photographing on top of the film. If the object is inanimate, connect a ground wire to it. Flick the switch SW1 about 10-20 times. Each time the switch is flicked you should see a discharge between the object and the discharge plate. This is what is being recorded on the film. Proper exposure is determined by trial and error, making adjustments accordingly.
Often times one uses B/W sheet film to determine the proper exposure then switch to a color sheet film for the final shoot. Black and White ortho has the advantages of being able to work using a red safe light and easily developed.
If you would like to use a standard 35mm camera or video camera to record Kirlian pictures it is possible, but you need to construct a transparent electrode.
The 35mm camera should be a single lens reflex (SLR) type with one or two close-up (macro) lens. In addition the camera needs to have a Bulb (B) setting on its shutter speeds. The B setting keeps the shutter open for as long as the shutter is held down.
Photographers typically use a cable release connected to the shutter to make B exposures. Most cable releases can be set to keep the shutter close when first pressed. The cable has a release mechanism the photographer hits to release the cable and allow the shutter to close after the exposure is made.
Kirlian Photography using a Transparent Discharge Plate
To take Kirlian photographs using a standard 35mm film camera, digital camera or video camera, requires the use of a transparent discharge plate.
To shoot Kirlian Photographs using a transparent discharge plate, the object is placed on one side and the camera on the other. The high voltage is applied to the transparent discharge plate and the camera shoots through the transparent discharge to capture the corona discharge around the object, see figure 5 below.
A transparent discharge plate is constructed from three main components; a 4" x 5" piece of glass that has a transparent conductive coating (typically tin oxide) on one side, see parts list, a ring electrode made from thin copper sheeting or thin one sided copper clad board, and a .005 thick piece of transparent plastic. The transparency of our transparent discharge plates surpasses 90 percent.
The conductive tin oxide coating on the glass is delicate. The high voltages involved in Kirlian photography can easily vaporize the coating. To prevent this from happening care must be exercise in the construction. If a solid electrical connection is not made between the tin oxide coating and the HV source the connection will spark between the HV conductor and tin oxide coating, vaporizing the tin oxide coating in the process. This renders the electrode useless. Second a single point connection to the tin oxide coating doesn't work for the same reason. The point source of the connection initiates a spark then continues to travel vaporization the tin oxide coating. It's a little interesting to watch. Starting from the single point connection and continuing vaporizing the tin oxide until the HV source can no longer spark (jump) across the distance. So soldering a wire directly to the tin oxide will not work.
The solution is to make ring electrode that makes contact with the tin oxide coating on all of the glass edges. This is a large surface area electrode. A conductive silver epoxy is used to make a strong mechanical and electrical connection between the ring electrode and the tin oxide glass electrode.
Making a Transparent Discharge Plate
Constructing A Transparent Electrode As stated previously, the transparent discharge plate is constructed from three main components; a 4" x 5" piece of glass with a transparent conductive tin oxide coating on one side, a rectangle ring electrode made from thin adhesive copper foil, that is 1/4 inch wide and 2 mils thick, and two .005 thick pieces of transparent plastic, as shown in Figure 2 below.
Figure 2
To make a small 4" x 5" transparent electrode, purchase the 4" x 5" tin oxide glass (Figure 3). When you receive the glass, be careful. The edges of the glass are sharp. It will be impossible to tell visually which side of the glass has the conductive tin oxide coating.
So, to determine the tin oxide coating side of the glass you need an ohmmeter. Inexpensive VOM's (volt-ohm-meters) are available from local electrical store. Set the VOM to read OHMS on lowest resistance setting. Bring both probes in contact with one side of the transparent electrode material. The side with the conductive coating will deflect the meter, see figure below. The meter shown above(Figure 4) is on its lowest resistance setting and is showing a resistance of 165 ohms per inch.
The electrode is made from thin adhesive copper foil. You can easily cut this material with a scissors. Begin by cleaning the tin oxide glass, thoroughly so you have a good surface for the copper foil to adhere to. Re-clean the surface throughout the assembly process to ensure that no prints or lint are left on the glass.
The outside dimension of the ring electrode is the same size as the tin oxide glass, with a tab running off on one end. To accomplish this, cut two 4 inch strips and two 5 inch strips of copper foil, equal to the length and width of the tin oxide glass. Remove the adhesive backing and place foil along the edge of the conductive side of the glass, as shown in Figure 5. Cut a one inch piece of copper foil and adhere it the lower right side of the glass. Fold any excess foil in half to create the tab, see Figure 6.
Test assembly with an ohmmeter before proceeding. Touch the tab of the ring electrode with one probe and touch the tin oxide coating with the other. You should show good conductivity (low resistance). Next, solder approximately 18 inches of high voltage wire to the protruding tab. the wire should lay perpendicular to the copper tab as shown in Figure 7. Try to solder the wire so that it lays as close to the glass as possible. This will connect the transparent discharge plate to the HV source when you are finished.
Figure 7
Since the conductive coating of the transparent electrode is delicate we need to protect it. Purchase two sheets of transparent plastic which is approximately .005 thick. Cut the plastic sheet to size adding 3/4 inch to all sides of the transparent electrode. Run a thin bead of clear silicone adhesive around the edge of the transparent electrode, on top of copper foil, as shown in Figure 8. Center the plastic on top of the conductive tin oxide coating (Figure 9). Place a book or flat object onto the transparent electrode to hold the assembly tight and together while the silicone cures. Curing time is approximately 12 - 24 hours.
Once the silicone is dry, repeat this process on the opposite side of glass by running a bead of silicone around the edge of glass and centering second piece of plastic on top of glass, as demonstrated in Figures 10 and 11. Place a book or flat object onto the transparent electrode to hold the assembly tight and together while the silicone cures. Wait another 12 -24 hours.
You should now have the transparent electrode sandwiched between two sheets of transparent plastic. Next, fill the gap from the edge of the glass to the outside edge of the plastic sheets with silicone adhesive, see figure 12. Place a book or flat object onto the transparent electrode once again to hold the assembly tight and together while the silicone cures. Allow to dry completely before continuing.
Create a frame for your transparent electrode by running black electrical tape along the edges of the transparent electrode to further secure the plastic sheets together (see Figure 14).
First, run the tape along the edges of the transparent electrode. When you reach the edge that has the HV wire protruding from the side, use an Exacto knife to cut a slice in the tape, so that the tape can be secured to electrode without leaving a gap. Figures 14 -18 demonstrate this process.
Then, wrap it around the glass on each side to cover the space between the edge of the electrode and where the copper foil appears, as demonstrated in Figures 19 and 20.
The tape serves a dual purpose. First, it helps secure the plastic sheets to the transparent electrode. Secondly, it provides insulation along the edge of the electrode where it is needed. You may want to double tape the corner edges to provide an additional measure of insulation.
Using a Transparent Discharge Plate
The transparent electrode may be used with a variety of High Voltage power supplies provided that it has an external HV lead to connect to the transparent electrode. We take the HV using an alligator clip wire connecting to the wire or copper electrode of the high voltage power supply and connect it to the tab of the transparent electrode. Typically the camera is located on the opposite side of the working surface. Because the electrode is transparent, you can shoot through the electrode as if it were a pane of glass.
As an example, here is the procedure to shoot a leaf. In this example the leaf is at ground potential and the transparent electrode is at the HV potential. Make the set up in a room that can be made relatively light tight. The leaf is placed on a black non-reflecting, nonconductive surface. This improves background contrast. The transparent electrode is placed over the leaf. The thin plastic sheet side on the electrode is placed on the leaf. The leaf is connected to ground using by an alligator clip wire.
The camera is positioned over the assembly (see Figure above). The view through the camera should only show the object under the transparent electrode. This is accomplished with a close up lens (macro); a 4+ adapter or a reversing ring. The camera must be manually focused onto the object. If an auto-focus camera is being used set it to manual operation.
Open the aperture (f-stop) of the camera as wide as possible (2.0 or 2.4). If you are using a digital camera manually set the exposure to 10-20 seconds. If you are using a film camera, set the shutter to B (bulb) to make long timed exposures. With the shutter set to B, The shutter remains open as long as pressure is kept on the shutter. Using a shutter release cable attached to the camera will make taking pictures much easier. You can use any type of color film in the camera. I strongly advise using the fastest film available, either ISO 1000 or ISO 1600.
Manually focus the camera on the object with the room lights on. After the camera is focused, shut off all the room lights. Use a flashlight with a deep red filter to navigate around the room. When you are by the camera turn off the flashlight and open the shutter of the camera using the cable release. Now turn on the high voltage power supply. Keep the hv power supply for the length of the exposure you are making (10-20 seconds. Next release the shutter using the cable release. Turn on the room lights and set up your next picture.
Manual Kirlian Device
The technique is similar to the one mentioned above. Manually focus the camera on the object with the room lights on. After the camera is focused, shut off all the room lights. Use a flashlight with a deep red filter to navigate around the room. When you are by the camera turn off the flashlight and open the shutter of the camera using the cable release. Now pulse the switch on the manual Kirlian Device. Hit the switch 50 to 100 X to build up a strong image onto the film. Next release the shutter using the cable release. Turn on the room lights and set up your next picture.
One hint I'd like to pass along. If you are using a 35 mm film camera, start each roll of film with a few conventional pictures. This allows the photo-lab to align the frames properly on the machine. Tell the photo lab to print all frames, they may interpret the glowing outlines and corona discharges as picture errors and not print them.
The same technique described here for taking stills may also be used to film real time Kirlian video.
One may ground the object you are photographing in any number of ways. The grounding figures below illustrates two methods. The first illustration shows the ground wire directly attached to the object. When shooting like this many times the ground wire will be visible in the photograph. To remove the wire in the photograph I use the second method illustrated, a small hole is made in the black paper background. The ground wire is fed through the hole to touch the object being photographed. Resting the object on top of the ground wire, gives a nice black background and a ground for the object without any obtrusive wires. You may also use a small grounded copper plate in place of the wire as long as the object you are shooting makes contact with the plate through the hole of the black paper background.

Scifi Magazine 'Starlog' Relaunches, and Back Issues Return to Internet Archive (hollywoodreporter.com) 2
			
		 	
				Slashdot reader sandbagger brings news about the classic science ficdtion magazine Starlog.  It's 33-year run from 1976 to 2009 is now available on the Internet Archive.
And 17 years after publishing its final issue, Starlog is relaunching, publishing one issue a year starting November 13, according to The Hollywood Reporter:
The magazine launched in 1976 and gained a fanbase for being one of the few places to get reliable news and interviews about geek culture. It launched with a cover focusing on Star Trek, one of its key areas of expertise, but the magazine's editors and writers also pioneered coverage of Star Wars. (The new issue chronicles some of the rumors it put out a year before the movie opened). In all, Starlog published 374 issues and more than 25,000 pages...    In addition to the film and TV-focused features Starlog was known for, the new Starlog will also delve into futurism, as the cover from Gabriel Yeganyan highlights. The opening story of issue No. 1 looks at the viability of humans farming crops on planets outside of Earth, for example... The revival was announced earlier this year, and includes a podcast hosted by Jordan Hoffman, a veteran journalist who has contributed to The Hollywood Reporter.

I Use Development-Driven Specs, Not Spec-Driven Development
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-08
03:51:55 UTC
12:51:55 KST

Ensemble is the coding agent built from The Art of Building AI Coding Agents, Bill Cox's executable teaching book. Edition 3 is the current edition: the application is C# 14 / .NET 10, developed inside Ensemble with Cadence.
Build the code and the specification together. Development earns the spec. The goal is not to write a detailed plan up front and hand it to an AI, or to vibe-code a system and document its source afterward. The goal is to become the expert by pair-programming with the AI, then preserve that expertise in a form another agent can execute.
Bill's workflow is concrete:
- Discuss the design, then draft just the next chapter's TL;DR and exercises.
- Pair-program the implementation. Steer the AI in real time, try ideas, correct mistakes, and record what was unclear and why each important decision was made.
- Once the implementation passes the exercise graders, use those notes to write the chapter's body: the construction process, lessons, tradeoffs and reasons, not a tour of the finished source.
- Give a fresh student agent the chapter and graders to test whether the teaching is sufficient to reconstruct the implementation. Feed what that attempt reveals back into the chapter.
The result is an executable specification: a codebook that transfers the judgment needed to build the system, not merely a list of desired features. Ensemble V2 executed this book to build V3; Bill and Cadence then worked inside V3 to improve both the agent and the teaching. Individual chapters retain their documented verification limits.
The human's expertise grows during development instead of depending on a final inspection of unfamiliar generated code. The code is an output; the reusable asset is the knowledge needed to build it again. Don't just document what to build. Teach how and why to build it that way. You cannot learn all of that up front.
- Read the book, chapter guide, or assembled manuscript.
- Current application source and human companion.
- Preserved chapter solutions.
- Promotion and verification, implementation evidence.
- Frozen history: Edition 1 and Edition 2, each with its book, source and graders.
Use an existing .NET 10 SDK (the runtime alone cannot build the application). Nothing here installs an SDK, reads credentials for a build, starts a server automatically, or replaces an installed application.
First check that your shell can find the SDK:
dotnet --list-sdks           # should include a 10.x SDK
If dotnet is not found, or make reports dotnet: No such file or directory,
the build has not reached compilation: the SDK executable is missing from your
shell's PATH. If you already have the SDK, either add its directory to PATH
for the current shell (replace the example path with your actual SDK directory):
export PATH="/absolute/path/to/dotnet-sdk:$PATH"
dotnet --list-sdks
Or leave PATH unchanged and give Make the executable's full path:
make DOTNET=/absolute/path/to/dotnet
make cli DOTNET=/absolute/path/to/dotnet
# Use the same override for other targets, such as make test.
For example, if you installed the SDK in $HOME/.local/share/ensemble-dotnet10,
use make DOTNET="$HOME/.local/share/ensemble-dotnet10/dotnet". This is an example
location, not a required layout. If no .NET 10 SDK is installed, install one from
Microsoft's .NET 10 downloads,
then open a new terminal and check again.
With dotnet on PATH:
make                         # builds the C# GUI and writes ./ensemble launcher
make cli                     # builds the C# terminal client
./ensemble --help            # explicit launch; inspect options before use
# Terminal client after make cli:
dotnet agent/Cli/bin/Debug/net10.0/ch04.dll --help
If you used the explicit SDK override instead, use that full executable path
in place of dotnet when launching the terminal client too.
Choose your own data directory, workspace, funding configuration and unused port for a real launch. Do not replace a running host just to refresh source. The GUI embeds its assets at build time. See the crossover chapter and the source README for application behavior and qualified test evidence.
make test runs the C# regression executable and root Go tooling tests;
make test-go tests only the latter. Go remains at the root for reusable graders,
local fake providers and publication tooling—not as the active runtime.
Use each chapter's documented executable command and scope. C# must not be sent
to a Go-source consumer grader. For example, Chapter 5 documents the explicit
grade-edition3 -ch 5 -executable-only route and its 65 scoped points.
make grade CH=5 DIR=/path/to/CLI-wrapper GRADEFLAGS='-executable-only ...'
requires an explicit target and forwards to that Edition 3 command.
Executable grader numbers and preserved chNN snapshots retain their original
meanings; the chapter mapping
explains later manuscript renumbering. Historical Edition 2 grading can be run
from edition-2/; Edition 1 remains in edition-1/. Neither archive is rewritten
for the current layout.
make book assembles the current manuscript; make ebook builds its EPUB;
make print builds its print and reading PDFs. These use existing local publishing
tools, never install them. See book build instructions
for prerequisites and isolated output. Generated publications are distinct from
source edits; a source promotion alone does not publish a new book or upload it.
The Puffin book is the working home for Gateway,
Connections, Agenda, Puffin and the related Watchtower/security work. Its notes
follow Development-Driven Specs; outlines are provisional, not completed features.
The coding-agent book remains under book/.

Sub-2-BPW Structural Compression for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs
Compressing Gemma 4 26B-A4B from 9.92 GB to 4.75 GB (10.95B physical container params at 3.47 BPW, 1.48 effective bits/param relative to unpruned 25.6B base), enabling 100% In-VRAM GPU Execution on 6 GB laptop GPUs with 45.4 Tokens/Second throughput.
Author: Aman Sachan (Google Scholar | amansachan92905@gmail.com)
Organization: Independent Systems Research
Model Focus:
This 4.75 GB checkpoint is profiled and pruned specifically for Python programming, algorithm synthesis, data structures, and formal STEM reasoning.
By excising 80 dormant multilingual and general conversational experts, the active 48-expert manifold preserves deductive reasoning and syntax generation while fitting entirely within consumer 6 GB VRAM.
The MAPLE pruning pipeline is domain-agnostic and can be applied to any specialized field (such as medicine, finance, legal analysis, or creative writing) using the scripts in this repository:
- Calibration: Run engine/maple_profile.py with your custom text corpus (e.g., engine/calibration_corpus.txt or a proprietary dataset) to profile routing frequency and activation energy via llama-imatrix.
- Selection: MAPLE scores experts according to the composite energy metric and outputs engine/expert_selection_map.json.
- Container Assembly: Run engine/maple_build.py to slice the unpruned base model and assemble an optimized sub-2-BPW GGUF container for 100% in-VRAM edge execution.
The pre-built, verified 4.75 GB GGUF container is hosted on Hugging Face:
- Hugging Face Model Repository: AmSach/Gemma-4-26B-A4B-MAPLE-Sub5GB-GGUF
- Direct Binary Download: gemma-4-26B-A4B-MAPLE-IQ2_XXS.gguf (4.75 GB)
llama-cli \
  -hf AmSach/Gemma-4-26B-A4B-MAPLE-Sub5GB-GGUF \
  -ngl 99 \
  -c 2048 \
  -n 512 \
  --temp 0.4 \
  --top-p 0.9 \
  --repeat-penalty 1.1 \
  -r "<turn|>" \
  -p "<|turn>user\nExplain why the sky is blue using Rayleigh scattering.\n<turn|>\n<|turn>model\n"
(llama-cli downloads the 4.75 GB GGUF container directly from Hugging Face and allocates all 30 layers into GPU VRAM at 45.4 TPS).
While global multilingual pre-training necessitates 128–256 experts to serve hundreds of human languages and encyclopedic domains, localized edge deployments (such as English technical reasoning and code generation) activate only a narrow subset of experts.
MAPLE discovers this latent manifold by profiling live forward-pass expert activations via llama-imatrix, scoring experts via a composite frequency-energy metric:
$$s_{e,l} = C_{e,l} \cdot \sqrt{\bar{\sigma}_{e,l} + \epsilon}$$
where:
- s_{e,l}: Composite importance score for expert e in layer l
- C_{e,l}: Empirical token activation frequency (routing count)
- σ̄_{e,l}: Mean input channel activation energy
- ε: Numerical stability floor (10⁻⁸)
Applied to Gemma 4 26B-A4B (128 routed experts, 30 layers), MAPLE excises 80 dormant experts per layer to assemble an optimized 48-expert GGUF container capturing 79.88% of active forward-pass activation mass.
VRAM Accounting Note:
The 4.75 GB GGUF container occupies 4,526 MiB of static weight memory in VRAM. With a 2048-token context window KV cache and compute scratch buffers (~294 MiB), total runtime memory allocation is 4,820 MiB, leaving 1,324 MiB (1.29 GiB) of free VRAM headroom on a 6 GB (6,144 MiB) RTX 4050 GPU.
Bitrate Accounting Note:
4,745,987,168 bytes across the 10,952,640,446 physical tensor elements evaluates to 3.47 physical bits/param (blended between IQ2_XXS on experts and higher precision on shared layers). Relative to the unpruned 25.6B base architecture, it represents 1.48 effective bits per base parameter.
- 
The Ghost Router (Proxy-Embedding Failure): Estimating routing by projecting Layer-0 token embeddings through deep router gates failed because non-linear attention shifts rotate deep hidden representations ($l \ge 10$ ) into orthogonal spaces. MAPLE enforces true forward profiling viallama-imatrix.
- 
Cascaded Normalization Suppression: Multiplying expert scales by $0.795$ to counteract softmax denominator shrinkage compounded across 30 layers ($0.795^{30} \approx 0.001$ ), blinding the residual stream. Scales must be preserved at$1.0\times$ .
- 
DRY Sampler Interference: The --dry-allowed-length 2 penalty broke Python syntax by penalizing mandatory 2-character keywords (def, if, in, +=). Standard nucleus sampling restored syntax fidelity.
maple/
├── README.md                           # Documentation & benchmarks
├── LICENSE                             # Apache 2.0 License
├── requirements.txt                    # Python dependencies
├── run_maple.bat                       # Interactive Windows launcher
├── upload_to_hf.py                     # Hugging Face Hub upload utility
└── engine/
    ├── maple_profile.py                # Scores & extracts top-48 experts from imatrix
    ├── maple_build.py                  # Slices & builds 4.75 GB GGUF container
    ├── benchmark.py                    # Automated 4-domain semantic fidelity suite
    ├── chat.py                         # Streaming console with live telemetry
    ├── calibration_corpus.txt          # Multi-domain calibration corpus (1,979 tokens)
    └── expert_selection_map.json       # Extracted 30-layer empirical expert indices
pip install -r requirements.txt
On Windows:
run_maple.bat
Or via Python:
python engine/chat.py
The compressed 4.75 GB GGUF model container is publicly hosted on Hugging Face:
- Repository: https://huggingface.co/AmSach/Gemma-4-26B-A4B-MAPLE-Sub5GB-GGUF
- Direct Binary (4.75 GB): gemma-4-26B-A4B-MAPLE-IQ2_XXS.gguf
@article{sachan2026maple,
  title={MAPLE: Manifold-Activated Pruning of Latent Experts},
  author={Sachan, Aman},
  journal={Zenodo},
  year={2026},
  doi={10.5281/zenodo.24042480},
  url={https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24042480}
}

ISponsorBlockTV: SponsorBlock client for all YouTube TV clients
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-08
03:47:19 UTC
12:47:19 KST

iSponsorBlockTV is a self-hosted application that connects to your YouTube TV app (see compatibility below) and automatically skips segments (like Sponsors or intros) in YouTube videos using the SponsorBlock API. It can also auto mute and press the "Skip Ad" button the moment it becomes available on YouTube ads.
Warning
YouTube appers to have changed the screen ID code format and is in the process of revoking all existing codes. This means that you'll have to pair your device again. You can find information on how to do that in the wiki.
Check the wiki
Legend: ✅ = Working, ❌ = Not working, ❔ = Not tested
Open an issue/pull request if you have tested a device that isn't listed here.
*Ad muting won't work when using AirPlay to send the audio to another speaker.
** Shorts aren't fully supported due to limitations on YouTube's side. A single short can be seen by either selecting the "Disconnect" option in the warning shown or by long pressing the thumbnail to open the menu and clicking play from there
Run iSponsorBlockTV on a computer that has network access. It doesn't need to be on the same network as the device, only access to youtube.com is required.
Auto discovery will require the computer to be on the same network as the device during setup. The device can also be manually added to iSponsorBlockTV with a YouTube TV code. This code can be found in the settings page of your YouTube TV application.
- pyytlounge Used to interact with the device
- asyncio and aiohttp
- async-cache
- Textual Used for the amazing new graphical configurator
- ssdp Used for auto discovery
- Fork it (https://github.com/dmunozv04/iSponsorBlockTV/fork)
- Create your feature branch (git checkout -b my-new-feature)
- Commit your changes (git commit -am 'Add some feature')
- Push to the branch (git push origin my-new-feature)
- Create a new Pull Request
Made with contrib.rocks.

====================================================================================================

Goodbye Claude, Hello Pi
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-08
03:11:48 UTC
12:11:48 KST

I’ve been a longtime Claude Code user, ever since it became available in the Claude Pro subscription plan over a year ago. I remember the first time I used it, feeling giddy as it spat out messy Python code for whatever random weekend project I was working on, all from a single prompt. I’m certain that in a few years we’ll all look back at its release as one of the major drivers of this new era of software development we’re in.
Despite this, due to an accumulation of many small frustrations, I’ve finally decided to retire it as my main coding harness and migrate my development setup to use Pi instead.
Someone at Anthropic must’ve caught wind… just a day after making my decision, they announced Claude Code would finally start supporting AGENTS.md files in an effort to get me to stay. Unfortunately, for me it was too little, too late.
In lieu of a proper obituary, I’d like to take this time to say a few words about the things I liked least about Claude Code (and thus what I’m looking forward to avoiding with Pi).
The AGENTS.md thing kind of epitomizes the whole reason I decided to quit using Claude Code. Don’t get me wrong, having a ton of CLAUDE.md files scattered about various GitHub hobby repos is great guerrilla marketing. But it sucks for users to have to deal with copying or symlinking between the two formats.
Like the Luddite I am, I still prefer to review most of the code it churns out before I commit it. I use(d) Claude Code inside VSCode, and it lets you connect the harness to the IDE. This is nice because you can highlight text in a file and have it automatically included in the context when asking questions or requesting changes during a review.
Except you can only have one session “connected” to the IDE at a time. If you open a second tab to start another task while the first one works, it can’t receive the text selection events from the IDE at all. This has been reported at least as far back as December 2025… and closed as a duplicate of a totally unrelated bug.1
Of course, you can always use the official Claude Code VSCode GUI extension, which does allow you to have multiple tabs open and load the text selection into the context of each. But this extension has plenty of problems of its own. Half of the built-in slash commands don’t work at all in the GUI. It doesn’t have any support for a custom status line. Subagent output messages are simply layered into the main chat thread in chronological order, which is a mess to read when more than one is running. I could go on and on.
In spite of all of these existing issues, it seems like Anthropic has worked at a feverish pace to pack more features into Claude Code without much care for quality or stability. Dynamic workflows, agent teams, ultrareviews – all great ways to help enterprise customers burn ever more tokens each day, but not really anything I want or need.
Alongside all of these feature releases, they always seem to be fiddling with things in ways that mess up my personal setup. At the time of writing, there have been 244 releases to Claude Code this year; over 3/4 of these changed the system prompt or tool descriptions, which can substantially change how the LLM performs (especially when these new instructions contradict user prompts or repo conventions).
And what are all those changes for? Despite chopping 80% of the system prompt, Claude Code still has way more overhead than Pi for seemingly no benefit. The default Bash tool’s description is almost 3,000 tokens, most of which is random stuff about using git and opening PRs on GitHub.2 The models already know how to do this without detailed instructions, by the way.3
This post has been a lot of hating. I’m sorry! That’s not my usual style, and I do think Anthropic has made a really cool piece of software here. I’m also not under any delusion that I would’ve done a better job if I were the one in charge of running Claude Code from the beginning. LLM coding harnesses are a very new space and being one of the first to innovate in a field is a really hard task. Later tools like Pi have the benefit of hindsight to see what works and what doesn’t, what’s a core feature and what can be cut.
Ultimately, CC feels like a product without a strong vision or direction. It’s as if a bunch of excited engineers and PMs are rapidly tacking on whatever features suit their workflow… but without consideration for how this might make things worse for other users. It seems like there isn’t someone high-up whose job is strictly to say “no” to these requests, which is incredibly important when it comes to product engineering. So, for now, I’m leaving.
I’m excited to lean more into Pi. The first few days required some finagling, but since then it’s been smooth sailing. I’m enjoying the stability, minimalist approach, and the extreme level of customizability. I’ve already written a couple of extensions to support my use cases, which I’m hoping to share soon after I’ve cleaned them up a bit. If you’re also feeling these frustrations, I’d highly encourage giving an open source harness a try!
It’s not like there’s some huge technical roadblock to this either. After installing Pi, I wrote an extension to handle this very functionality across multiple open tabs in like 20 minutes. (return)
Bonus points for the fact that it instructs the model to use cat <<'EOF' which (prior to widespread rollout of auto mode) would always trip the manual review rules regardless of existing git command allowlists. (return)
If you assume 1 million DAU and 100M requests per day, trimming 1,000 tokens off of this could literally save millions of dollars in inference costs per year. Surely that’s worth some engineer’s time?? (return)

ZapFast: A fast, native WhatsApp client in Rust and egui
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-08
02:02:27 UTC
11:02:27 KST

WhatsApp, native and fast.
A lightweight WhatsApp app for Linux, macOS, and Windows.
Download · Getting started · User guide · Contribute
ZapFast is a WhatsApp client written in Rust with egui and fastframe, the framework behind a family of native desktop apps. It links to your phone as a companion device through whatsapp-rust and has no browser engine. In our Linux test it opens in under a second and uses about 200 MB of idle RAM, against 1.13 GB for WhatsApp Web. See the measurements.
The interface is available in English, Chinese (Simplified and Traditional), Dutch, French, German, Indonesian, Italian, Portuguese (Brazil), Russian, Spanish, and Turkish.
Watch ZapFast in action
zapfast-launch-web.mp4
Features · Install · Link your phone · Help
- Open ZapFast. It shows a QR code.
- On your phone, open WhatsApp, then Settings, Linked devices, Link a device, and scan the code. Or click Link with phone number and enter the eight-character code on your phone.
- Your recent chats arrive from the phone. Older messages follow as you scroll up.
The link survives restarts, and your phone does not need to stay online to read what ZapFast has already stored. See Getting started for the full walkthrough and How it links for the details.
The complete guide lives at zapfast.rocks.
Read CONTRIBUTING.md before opening an issue or pull request. To look at the interface without a WhatsApp account, run:
cargo run --features demo -- --demo
AGENTS.md describes the architecture, DEMO.md the demo pages, screenshots, and recorded tours, and PACKAGING.md releases and packages.
Native notifications use Fastframe on Linux, macOS and Windows. ZapFast keeps its account/chat routing, privacy settings, notification limits and sound previews. Clicks open the announced message; reading a chat withdraws its tracked notifications. OS permissions and notification presentation still apply.
Want Spotify just as fast and native? Spotifast is ZapFast's sibling. Both are built on fastframe.
ZapFast uses whatsapp-rust,
egui, fastframe, the
Inter typeface and Noto Color Emoji (OFL), and
Lucide icons (ISC), also used for the chat wallpaper
doodles. The notification sounds are Pidgin's, under the
GPL-2.0 (see assets/sounds/).
ZapFast is an unofficial client and is not affiliated with WhatsApp or Meta. Using an unofficial client may be against WhatsApp's terms of service and could get an account suspended. Use it at your own risk. WhatsApp is a trademark of WhatsApp LLC.
Licensed under the MIT License.

AlpineBSD Announced - an AI-free FOSS OS
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-08
00:46:00 UTC
09:46:00 KST

AlpineBSD Announced - an AI-free FOSS OS (theregister.com) 13
			
		 	
				Lproven (Slashdot reader #6,030) shares his article about a new OS in the BSD family "combining NetBSD's kernel and libc with a port of the Alpine Linux userland" — and promising it won't use AI-generated code:
 AlpineBSD is so new that it barely exists yet. There are no installation media available, though it's possible to manually bootstrap an installation. What AlpineBSD does already have is an AI policy, and the plan is to avoid "the output of generative AI models (e.g. LLMs)" as far as possible:
"Any software whose code, documentation, binaries, or other assets include, in any part, the output of a generative AI tool such as an LLM, is considered 'tainted' for our purposes...." 
Back in 2024, the NetBSD project made its position on AI plain. Its commit guidelines say that "code generated by a Large Language Model [...] is presumed to be tainted code...."
AlpineBSD's ports repository and source code are hosted on Sourcehut, under the account of sircmpwn, better known as Drew DeVault...   We will follow AlpineBSD with interest. There's strong appeal in the plan of combining perhaps the best-known, longest-established AI-free Unix-like kernel with the user-facing parts of pretty much the smallest and cleanest Linux distro there is. The AI advocates often warn LLM skeptics that they risk being left behind. Well, here's an OS for those who don't want to surf the AI wave.  
The article does acknowledge difficulty installing software using the pkgin package manager (an optional extra), explaining that since 1993 the NetBSD community has had "its own ideas about what is normal... Some folks on the mailing list told us that they never use binary packages anyway — they compile what they need from source code."

Microsoft Removes PC-to-PC Migration from Windows 11, Points Users to OneDrive-Based Backup
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
23:35:00 UTC
08:35:00 KST

Microsoft Removes PC-to-PC Migration from Windows 11, Points Users to OneDrive-Based Backup (ghacks.net) 43
			
		 	
				Windows 11 offered a PC-to-PC Migration feature that "moved files and settings directly between two PCs over Wi-Fi or Ethernet," reports Ghacks. But according to an updated Microsoft support page, the tool is no longer available.
"Microsoft's support documentation now redirects users to Windows Backup, a tool that relies on OneDrive cloud storage instead of a direct connection between two computers..."
Windows Backup works differently. It requires a personal Microsoft account and backs up supported folders (Desktop, Documents, Pictures, Videos and Music) to OneDrive.  Free OneDrive accounts include only 5GB of storage, and Microsoft's own promotional screenshot for Windows Backup shows 4.5GB of that allowance already in use. Years of photos, documents and downloads can exceed the free tier quickly.
Windows Backup does carry some extras. It can remember installed apps, Wi-Fi networks, passwords and some settings to speed up setup on a new device. Apps still have to be reinstalled, and supported files are restored from the cloud.
 
Thanks to Slashdot reader joshuark for sharing the article.

GitHub New pull request overview page in public preview
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
23:09:40 UTC
08:09:40 KST

New pull request overview page in public preview
On a long pull request, checking what’s blocking often means scrolling past dozens of comments and timeline events just to reach the merge box. And once you’re there, you still need to work out what to do next. As people need to review more and more pull requests, time spent scrolling adds up. So we’ve begun to rethink the entire experience, beginning with addressing a familiar challenge: finding what’s blocking progress and determining what to do next.
Today, we’re introducing the Overview page as an opt-in public preview, with rollout continuing through the weekend. It brings merge status and next available actions to the top of the page in a compact readiness summary. It’s the first step toward making pull requests easier to follow as code, conversations, and reviews evolve.
The merge box, distilled
We’re starting with the merge box, the place developers go to see whether a pull request can move forward. Rather than just moving it higher, we’ve also redesigned it to show the same information in less than half the space.
- Explore the supporting signals: A compact view of checks, comments, and approvals keeps the context nearby without making every detail compete for attention.
- Address what’s blocking: Take action to move the pull request forward, such as updating your branch.
- See if it’s ready to merge: Check merge readiness and merge when requirements are met.
Start with the summary, then follow the detail that matters to your next step (e.g., get to failing checks, see who still needs to approve, or open the relevant comments).
Keep your place. Focus your attention.
This first preview also introduces more ways to catch up on activity and focus the conversation so you can spend less time finding your place.
- Narrow the conversation: Filter for unresolved conversations or comments you wrote to find open questions or follow up on your feedback.
- Read the latest comments first: Sort comments from newest to oldest instead of starting with the earliest discussion.
- Catch up on activity: Use the summary in the upper-right to find review comments, review requests, and other updates.
You can also mark a draft ready for review or close a pull request from the header, with available actions depending on your permissions.
Try the new overview
To join, select the ✨ Try the Overview page button in the header. We’re opening this preview early so you can try the new direction on your own pull requests and help shape what comes next. You can use the same menu to return to the classic overview during the preview.
Help shape what comes next
This preview is very much a starting point, not the finished pull request experience. Try it on your next pull request. What is easier to find? Where do you still lose context? What sends you back to the classic overview?
Share your feedback and report issues in the overview preview feedback discussion.

====================================================================================================

GitHub New pull request overview page in public preview
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
23:09:40 UTC
08:09:40 KST

New pull request overview page in public preview
On a long pull request, checking what’s blocking often means scrolling past dozens of comments and timeline events just to reach the merge box. And once you’re there, you still need to work out what to do next. As people need to review more and more pull requests, time spent scrolling adds up. So we’ve begun to rethink the entire experience, beginning with addressing a familiar challenge: finding what’s blocking progress and determining what to do next.
Today, we’re introducing the Overview page as an opt-in public preview, with rollout continuing through the weekend. It brings merge status and next available actions to the top of the page in a compact readiness summary. It’s the first step toward making pull requests easier to follow as code, conversations, and reviews evolve.
The merge box, distilled
We’re starting with the merge box, the place developers go to see whether a pull request can move forward. Rather than just moving it higher, we’ve also redesigned it to show the same information in less than half the space.
- Explore the supporting signals: A compact view of checks, comments, and approvals keeps the context nearby without making every detail compete for attention.
- Address what’s blocking: Take action to move the pull request forward, such as updating your branch.
- See if it’s ready to merge: Check merge readiness and merge when requirements are met.
Start with the summary, then follow the detail that matters to your next step (e.g., get to failing checks, see who still needs to approve, or open the relevant comments).
Keep your place. Focus your attention.
This first preview also introduces more ways to catch up on activity and focus the conversation so you can spend less time finding your place.
- Narrow the conversation: Filter for unresolved conversations or comments you wrote to find open questions or follow up on your feedback.
- Read the latest comments first: Sort comments from newest to oldest instead of starting with the earliest discussion.
- Catch up on activity: Use the summary in the upper-right to find review comments, review requests, and other updates.
You can also mark a draft ready for review or close a pull request from the header, with available actions depending on your permissions.
Try the new overview
To join, select the ✨ Try the Overview page button in the header. We’re opening this preview early so you can try the new direction on your own pull requests and help shape what comes next. You can use the same menu to return to the classic overview during the preview.
Help shape what comes next
This preview is very much a starting point, not the finished pull request experience. Try it on your next pull request. What is easier to find? Where do you still lose context? What sends you back to the classic overview?
Share your feedback and report issues in the overview preview feedback discussion.

Making any integer with four 2s (2025)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
22:38:58 UTC
07:38:58 KST

There's a cute math puzzle that can be interesting to folks on very different levels:
Given exactly four instances of the digit 2 and some target natural number,
use any mathematical operations to generate the target number with these 2s,
using no other digits.
Some examples can be done by elementary school kids:
In middle school, kids learn about exponents, factorials, etc. which expands the range considerably:
Then come the tricks; for example, the number 22 (twenty two) can be seen as a valid use of two 2s, and so on; so we can have:
Getting to 7 is notoriously difficult, but if you allow even more mathematical tools like the Gamma function, it becomes easy:
The more math skill people have, the more numbers they can make. See this thread for some fun concoctions using integrals, repeating fractions and combinatorial operators. One of my favorite examples involves complex numbers:
So the fun doesn't end even after one graduates from university! In fact, this seems to have been a favorite pastime for mathematicians in the 1920s. Until Paul Dirac ruined it for everyone by finding a general solution for every number.
It's all about nested square roots:
If the square root is applied n times:
All that's left now is some base-2 logarithms:
And another:
This leads to the general formula:
There's just one small wrinkle: it uses three instances of the digit 2, not four. This is easy to amend, however; since , we can replace any single digit with that and get exactly four:
One may claim this is cheating, but it seems to be in line with the rules of the puzzle! Note that the entity n doesn't actually appear anywhere - it's just a helper to count the number of repeated square roots. For example, another way to express 7 is:
There are exactly four 2s, and this uses only reasonable, elemental math operations to do the calculation. It's clear that any number can be expressed this way; the only challenge is properly drawing all those square roots!
Credits
I've read about this story in Graham Farmelo's book The Strangest Man: The Hidden Life of Paul Dirac, Quantum Genius. I'm enjoying this book so far.

Data Centers are Being Powered By Giant Gas Plants, Possibly Increasing Carbon Pollution for Decades
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
22:15:00 UTC
07:15:00 KST

Data Centers are Being Powered By Giant Gas Plants, Possibly Increasing Carbon Pollution for Decades (msn.com) 72
			
		 	
				Rather than racing toward their net-zero targets, Amazon and other AI/cloud computing companies "are driving emissions sharply higher," reports the Washington Post:
To support their data centers, tech firms are driving construction of a new generation of supersized, gas-fired power plants that could lock in hundreds of millions of tons of climate pollution for decades to come, according to corporate disclosures and an analysis conducted for The Post by a researcher at the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health.   Industry estimates show these projects have put the tech sector on course to grow from a modest driver of industrial carbon pollution into one of the world's biggest emitters of greenhouse gases. Some anticipate that its share of global carbon emissions will increase as much as fivefold from 2020 to 2030. That would leave the tech industry churning out more than half the climate pollution created by global aviation or shipping — sectors long associated with driving warming...
Already, Amazon's carbon footprint reached 80.85 million metric tons last year — 58% larger than when [Amazon CEO Jeff] Bezos pledged to shrink it. The company has veered far from the path laid out in the Paris agreement, which would have emissions falling steeply by now and dropping to 43% below 2019 levels by 2030.   The tech companies say in corporate disclosures and public statements that they remain committed to their net zero targets, and they suggest the emissions surge is temporary — to be mitigated down the road, when new energy technologies enable the data centers to run cleaner and more efficiently.  They also stress that they continue to invest in clean energy and rank as some of the world's biggest consumers of it. With some complicated accounting maneuvers and carbon credit purchases, their solar, wind and geothermal installations partly cancel out their gas emissions, according to corporate disclosures. 
A June report from international insurance/asset management Allianz found the world's data center emissions are on track to double to 643 million tons of CO2 annually between 2020 and 2030, the article points out.   And they cite American data centers contributing to the problem:
- Just two gas projects Amazon signed on to for data centers planned in Texas and Pennsylvania "would produce more carbon pollution than the dirtiest facility in the country," an Alabama coal plant powering 1 million homes.
- Meta is building a nearly six-square-mile campus in Louisiana "that will consume as much electricity as three cities the size of New Orleans."
- "A sprawling Microsoft project in West Texas involving a giant gas plant built by Chevron and a Google project in Wyoming that would consume five times as much electricity as every household in that state would vastly expand the emissions associated with those companies."
- "A data center campus in Pike County, Ohio, that will support OpenAI's artificial intelligence operations will consume significantly more gas power, and, therefore, emit more carbon pollution, than the Microsoft and Google projects combined."

After the midterms, things are looking rocky for Elon Musk and data centers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
22:07:22 UTC
07:07:22 KST

Democrats’ historic victory in this week’s midterm elections could also have a dramatic effect on the tech industry.
In dozens of local ballot measures, voters rejected data centers. And even when they weren’t voting on the issue directly, they were electing politicians — like Texas’ new governor-elect Gina Hinojosa — who campaigned on the promise of restricting data centers.
On the latest episode of TechCrunch’s Equity podcast, Kirsten Korosec, Sean O’Kane, and I discussed what the midterms mean for the tech industry. Sean kicked it off by noting that Elon Musk “seems to have basically nothing out of donating half a billion dollars” to Republicans, while his companies are now headquartered “in a state that has, remarkably, turned about as blue as it could possibly turn.”
And Kirsten said that while data centers probably weren’t the main cause of the “blue wave,” they’ve become an “an increasingly grassroots issue, in part because of how hyperscalers and companies have gone about” getting their projects approved. And now, “we’re already seeing companies like OpenAI all of a sudden go, ‘Actually, we can change our policies.’”
Keep reading for a preview of our conversation, edited for length and clarity.
Sean O’Kane: First off, let’s talk about the fact that one of the biggest data center builders was also the largest donor to the Republican Party. Elon Musk seems to have gotten basically nothing out of donating half a billion dollars — maybe even more, by the time we see the final count — and in fact is now sitting there with all of his businesses in a state that has, remarkably, turned about as blue as it could possibly turn for the first time in a very long time, if ever.
That, I think, is the biggest thing hanging above a lot of this discussion. But, there were also a bunch of ballot measures up around the country that would have paved the way for data centers to get built faster. Almost all of them got shot down, or voted against. [Actually, many of the ballot measures would have imposed restrictions or moratoriums on data centers, and almost all of those measures passed.]
With [Democrats] in power, there is a consensus view, at least in the tech world, that this is going to mean we have a lot of roadblocks ahead for data centers, especially in places like Texas, where [you have] folks like the governor-elect [Gina] Hinojosa, who has spoken pretty openly about this being a campaign promise and something that she wants to focus on once she takes power, now that she’s beaten Greg Abbott.
It’s the thing that I think the tech world is maybe a little too focused on. I’m curious what you guys think about that.
Anthony Ha: I don’t necessarily think that they’re too focused on it, but I understand why it would feel a little bit disproportionate — the data center ballot measures, certainly there were a number of them, but it wasn’t something that was on the ballot everywhere.
And in different races, the dynamic might have been a bit different, but there was this undercurrent and sense that it seems to be an issue that reaches across the country, that is bipartisan in a lot of ways, but that the Democrats seem to have been more effective at harnessing that energy. And of course, there’s all kinds of reasons why the Democrats did so well on Tuesday, but it does seem like anti-AI and especially anti-data center sentiment was a big part of it. That seems like a pretty potent political force.
The next two years, [and] potentially beyond that, are going to be more difficult for tech companies in general, and hyperscalers, data center companies in particular. I also imagine that they have to be a little bit worried about: Is this going to continue to be an issue, not just with the people who got elected this year, but potentially in 2028 and beyond?
Kirsten Korosec: Absolutely. I don’t think that the data center issue is what brought everyone to the polls and led to the quote-unquote “blue wave.” However, I will say that data centers have been an increasingly grassroots issue, in part because of how hyperscalers and companies have gone about getting these data centers [approved] — very secretive, oftentimes requiring NDAs of a elected officials or appointed officials, not sharing information about the scale of a project in very environmentally sensitive areas, including in the Southwest, where there’s very much a water scarcity issue.
So I think that a lot of folks — Republicans, independents, and Democrats — all saw this as wrong. They were motivated by it, but I don’t think it was a singular issue.
Now, here’s the interesting thing. There was a lot of waving of hands about how [AI companies] can’t possibly operate under any other circumstances. And we’re already seeing companies like OpenAI all of a sudden go, “Actually, we can change our policies.” They just released what they call a compute campus policy blueprint. And guess what? They’re not going to require NDAs anymore. They’re going to share their plans and supposedly operate in less secrecy.
I think what this shows is that when you put up either a regulatory certainty in the marketplace, or at least signals of going in that direction, then corporations will respond. If you don’t do that, they will not necessarily do the thing that we hope that they will do.
Anthony: One thing I want to emphasize is that they’re calling it this [their] “compute campus policy.” And part of what that’s doing, beyond laying out these policy changes, is also an attempt to rebrand data centers, because they have become such a toxic phrase. So they’re not data centers anymore, they are compute campuses, and I’m curious, whether either of you think that’ll take.
Sean: I mean, this is ridiculous. Come on. It’s not going to change anything. It’s going to cause them more effort. For a company that likes to say that it’s no longer focused on side quests, OpenAI still loves to go in different directions a lot. And this is just the latest kind of thing where it’s just like: Why are you spending time on this at all? It doesn’t really seem like it’s going to move the needle.
Kirsten: I have a great idea for OpenAI. Because in the spirit of making threatening things seem cute, they should 100% create a compute campuses avatar, [a] cutesy thing, and then just present that instead of a power-, energy-sucking, water-sucking data center.
Anthony: And if these are just lovely compute campuses, they should just build their own offices there or work next door, see how that works out for them.
Something else that I’m wondering about is, to what extent the election is maybe the second part of a one-two punch. [We’ve just seen] a big shift in how Wall Street is reacting to a lot of these hyperscalers and AI companies and this sense that [investors] are not excited about the idea that they should be spending all this money building new data centers, spending all this money on compute. And so there’s a financial incentive not to build all these data centers.
I’m very curious how that’s going to play out. [From a regulatory standpoint,] it’s probably going to be harder to build data centers. Wall Street is probably not going to reward you for building data centers. But at the same time, to get to the scale that they want to get to, they are probably going to need to build data centers somewhere.

Show HN: An offline desktop companion to help maintain a healthy blink rhythm
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
21:47:47 UTC
06:47:47 KST

EyesRhythm uses your webcam to score how often and how steadily you blink
on long screen days. Poise shows which way you turned your head, so the
neck and shoulders get a change of direction. No account, and no video
leaving your machine.
See the last 5 minutes of your blink rhythm on a live graph.
Stays beside the work. Counts only while you are at the screen.
Why it exists
Your eyes work hard for you. Screens make them work worse.
Focused computer use often cuts blink rate in half. Sparse, uneven
blinking is a known driver of digital eye strain, dryness, grit,
fatigue, blurred vision, and it is easy not to notice until the eyes
already hurt. EyesRhythm is a
blink rate detector that sits
beside that work.
What rhythm means
Rate and regularity, together.
Rate is how often. Regularity is how steady. Enough blinks can still
arrive in bursts and droughts; a slow but even pattern still leaves
the surface dry. EyesRhythm folds both into the
Ocular Rhythm Index (0 to 100). Maximize your healthy
desk ORI, use practice daily, it helps to build muscle memory.
The companion
It watches, scores, and, if you ask, gently nudges.
ORI on a compact window. Alert is always on: Visual by default, a
quiet orange pulse, or Sound if you click A. Optional beep on each
blink. Practice is a two-minute coach so the habit is easier to feel.
Poise is the neck’s half of the same sit. The dial turns green where
you looked and stays red where you did not, so a still pose gets a
turn up, down, left, or right, and the neck and shoulders can relax
before you face the work again. A wellness companion, not a medical
device. Lasting discomfort belongs with an eye-care professional.
Get the highlights
A companion, not another dashboard.
Poise
A dial of where your head went today, in 30° slices. Green is where you looked. Red is a direction you skipped. Turning through those directions lets the neck and shoulders ease, then you come back to the screen.
Live blink rate
A webcam blink rate detector in a compact window. ORI, screen time, and look angle stay in view. Rate and regularity sit on Trace.
Visual or Sound
Alert stays on. Visual is a quiet orange pulse. Click A for Sound. Beep (B) is a separate click on each blink.
Beep on each blink
A tighter feedback loop. Optional click on every counted blink.
Practice
A countdown, then a guided two-minute session that paces blinks so the habit is easier to feel.
Dockable GUI
Open Settings and Reports beside the companion, then hide the panel to keep the window small.
Built for the desk
A small window next to your work. Camera preview stays put-away unless you open Lens.
We care about your privacy
100% local. Nothing leaves this computer.
The app stays on your computer from the first launch.
No account, no license key, no cloud, and no analytics.
Camera frames stay on this computer. Nothing is uploaded or kept as a
film of your face.
Today’s totals and preferences stay in a local folder, yours alone.
EyesRhythm 1.6.0. Your browser saves EyesRhythm-1.6.0.dmg (93 MB) or EyesRhythm-Setup-1.6.0.exe (85 MB). Drag the Mac app into Applications. On Windows,
run Setup.
Tested on Intel Core i7 and on Mac processors. We recommend this configuration.
FAQ
Questions, answered.
What is a blink rate detector?
A blink rate detector uses a camera to notice each blink and measure how often they happen, usually as blinks per minute. EyesRhythm does that on your Mac or Windows computer, then also looks at how evenly those blinks are spaced.
Does EyesRhythm work with a laptop webcam?
Yes. It uses the built-in camera on a Mac or Windows laptop, or a USB webcam. Sit as you usually work so the camera can see your eyes. The setup guide in the journal walks through the few things that help.
Does any video leave my computer?
No. Frames are read on this machine and discarded. There is no account, no license key, no cloud, and no analytics. Camera frames are not uploaded or kept as a film of your face.
How is this different from a blink counter?
A counter only totals blinks. Six blinks can still mean two very different minutes if they arrive in clumps with a long dry wait in the middle. EyesRhythm folds rate and regularity into one Ocular Rhythm Index, from 0 to 100.
What is Poise for?
Poise shows which way you turned your head today, in 30° slices. Green is where you looked most. Red is a direction you barely used. Long computer work parks the neck in one pose. Poise is there so you move it up, down, left, and right from time to time, which lets the neck and shoulders relax, then you look back at the screen. It is a reminder, not a treatment.
Is EyesRhythm a medical device?
No. It is a wellness companion for desk work, not a diagnosis or a treatment. Lasting discomfort or a change in vision belongs with an eye-care professional.
The developer behind EyesRhythm has spent more than two decades working as
a software developer. Years of long hours in front of computer screens
gradually took a toll on his eye health, leading to frequent dryness,
redness, and, at times, significant discomfort.
Advances in modern technology made it possible to create EyesRhythm: a
simple, lightweight companion designed to support healthier blinking
habits during extended screen use. The same hours also leave the neck
and shoulders holding one pose. Poise was added so the day includes
turning the head: the dial goes green where you looked and stays red
where you did not, a prompt to move, then return to the work. Having
personally used the application, he has found it useful and effective
enough to share with others.
Our goal is simple: to build technology that solves a real problem
without creating another distraction. EyesRhythm works quietly in the
background, helping users become more aware of their blink rhythm while
staying focused on their work.
We believe the best products are shaped by real experiences and honest
feedback. If you choose to try EyesRhythm, we would greatly appreciate
hearing about your experience at [email protected].
Your feedback will help us continue improving the product and ensure
that it remains simple, useful, reliable, and unobtrusive.

Megaspeed exec was recruited to spy on Nvidia CEO, according to US intelligence memo
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
21:17:08 UTC
06:17:08 KST

A memo circulated earlier this year claimed that technology executive Alice Huang was recruited by China’s Ministry of State Security to spy on Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, according to multiple media reports.
The New York Times, which had previously said Nvidia’s chief executive had been the target of Chinese intelligence, was the first to report on the memo, which was reportedly based on U.S. intelligence. The Financial Times subsequently reported that the memo also claimed Jensen Huang had a romantic relationship with Alice Huang, an executive at a Singapore-based company called Megaspeed.
An Nvidia spokesperson told the NYT that Jensen Huang’s interactions with Alice Huang were “not unusual,” and that the company does not share classified information with its customers.
A separate NYT report examined New Matrix, a Chinese company authorized by the U.S. Commerce Department to buy advanced Nvidia chips. But at the office address listed by New Matrix, reporters only found “a hollow gray shell,” raising concerns that it could be a front for a blacklisted private equity firm.
The Nvidia spokesperson said the company sells “the first, best and most of our technology in America.”
