NYT Cooking’s AI assistant saved my weeknight chicken, but almost ruined my cake
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:47:15 UTC
00:47:15 KST

Around 7PM each weeknight, I find myself standing in front of the fridge, trying to decide on something for dinner that won’t make my husband complain, “Chicken again?” Yes, I should meal plan more, but I don’t, so this is where I’m at. A nightly battle in which the chicken usually wins.
NYT Cooking’s AI assistant saved my weeknight chicken, but almost ruined my cake
I tested the new AI-powered tool on ingredient swaps, recipe scaling, and a missing Bundt pan.
Recently, my best weapon in this fight has been AI. Between Alexa Plus in the Echo smart display on my kitchen counter, the Samsung Food app on my fridge, and even an AI-powered cooking robot, AI has become my go-to sous chef — albeit one I need to keep a close eye on. It’s helped me swap ingredients, generate ideas, fix things when I mess up, and yes, even cook better. So I was intrigued when my go-to recipe app, NYT Cooking, introduced an AI assistant.
Many, including some NYT Cooking users and recipe purists, are skeptical of this new tool (available to subscribers on iOS only at launch), and I get why. One of the things I like most about NYT Cooking is that it features thousands of recipes from dozens of reputable sources — such as the New York Times archives, restaurants, cookbooks, and food columnists — and real people have tested them. (I’ve tried some purely AI-generated recipes and they were not good).
Another great feature of the service, which I’ve been paying for for several years, is the reader comments. Here, I’ve often found solutions to cooking dilemmas, like what to substitute for makrut lime leaves — there’s always one slightly bougie ingredient in an NYT Cooking recipe that I don’t have on hand.
However, reading through hundreds of comments to find answers at 7PM on a weeknight isn’t always practical. And this is where I’ve found the NYT Cooking assistant helpful. Among other things, it was trained on user comments, and in my few days testing it, it provided practical substitutions and solid cooking guidance for the new ingredients. It also won’t unquestionably agree to crazy requests (I tried!). As with all AI tools, it’s not perfect — it almost made me put half the required butter in my cake — but as a tool, it is useful and feels like a solid application of AI. Importantly, it doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you.
It doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you
The assistant appears in the app in a small text box on the recipe page with a prompt: “Customize this recipe…” From here, you type in a question to adapt it to your needs. This can be as simple as “What can I substitute for this ingredient?” to “Scale this recipe to feed seven people, and make it vegan and gluten-free.” The New York Times has also said the feature can suggest substitutions for cooking equipment and convert measurements; the latter option has been in the app for a couple of years.
Last Friday night, instead of staring aimlessly into the fridge, I turned to the Chicken folder in my NYT Cooking app Recipe Box and found a One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger I’d been wanting to try. Only I didn’t have chicken thighs or half the ingredients.
I could have figured these steps myself, but it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible.
I typed my dilemma into the assistant box on the recipe page: “I have chicken tenders, fresh ginger, garlic, rice, broth, limes, but no chard or cilantro or scallions. I do have green beans and red onions.” Within seconds, it created a new version of the recipe, with a new title: One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger and Green Beans. It suggested a shorter browning time for the tenders, cooking the red onion alongside the chicken, and steaming the green beans on top of the rice for the last eight minutes instead of the chard. Every change was highlighted in the new recipe under a Your Version tab, and I could flip back to the original at any point.
I noticed it hadn’t adjusted for the lack of cilantro, saying it was optional. So I asked if I could use Thai basil, which I happen to have, and it agreed, highlighting how it would change the flavor. While I could have figured out a lot of these steps myself, maybe with the help of Google, it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible and nice not to have to swipe out of the app. The NYT Cooking editors say it’s been trained not to comply with requests that will taste bad.
The resulting dish — which took the same amount of time to cook as the original — was delicious and got my husband’s seal of approval (he just had the leftovers for lunch). It was very different from the recipe Yasmin Fahr wrote, and I can see the purists’ point. But the rewritten recipe was much easier to use and produced a better dish than winging it with my own subs gleaned from the comments section, which I usually do with varying degrees of success.
I cook regularly, so I had a sense of what ingredients I had on hand that might work for the dish I wanted to make. But the assistant can also help if you have no idea what to use as a substitute. For example, I was cooking a Thai red curry and asked what I could use instead of makrut lime leaf. It replied, saying that grated lime zest would work, but “it misses the deeper floral, herbal aroma” of fresh limes. (See? Bougie.)
Over the weekend, I tried several more swaps, asking whether half-and-half would work instead of cream in a caramel apple Dutch baby, or if Castelvetrano olives and smoked paprika could stand in for kalamata and chile pepper in a roasted halibut dish. Swapping spices is always a bold move in any dish, as it can really ruin the flavor.
I also had the app convert the Dutch baby recipe’s metric weights to US measurements, which it did seamlessly. Then I threw in some curveballs. How about using fresh cayenne peppers with the halibut? It warned me that substituting cayenne for chile “brings significant, sharp heat.” Could I swap ladyfingers for minced beef in this trifle (à la Rachel Green in Friends)? “Minced beef cannot be used in place of the ladyfingers or anywhere in this sweet dessert trifle.” Good call.
While I was cooking, I compared the NYT Cooking assistant with Alexa Plus, Amazon’s generative-AI voice assistant I regularly use to help me cook via the Echo Show 11 smart display in my kitchen. Interestingly, it offered the same guidance as the Cooking assistant on substitutions for the chicken and rice dish, but with slightly different cooking times. Alexa pulls some recipes from the NYT app through a partnership between the companies.
However, Alexa couldn’t show the modified recipe on its screen; it could only tell me the changes while showing the original. When I asked it to walk me through them, it got confused and improvised, telling me to cut the chicken into bite-sized pieces — which wasn’t in the modified recipe. Overall, I found using the Cooking app more reliable, although messier. It would be great to be able to use voice control to navigate the app, as I can with Alexa on my smart display, rather than using my garlicky fingers.
Time for a bigger challenge: baking.
I am typically not a baker. My cakes come out flat, and my cookies don’t crumble. Even the smart mixer I tested a few years ago couldn’t save me. I also rarely have the right equipment. So I took Cinnamon Roll Bundt Cake, a recipe by NYT Cooking editor Genevieve Ko (who announced the AI feature), and asked it to turn it into muffins. (Who has a Bundt cake pan?!)
It modified the recipe’s cooking time from three hours to 50 minutes and split the batter across two 12-muffin tins. But that was going to make 24 muffins, way too many for me. So I asked it to halve the recipe to get 12 muffins. This led to some smart choices, like halving five eggs to three instead of an unhelpful two and a half.
For science, I bought a Bundt pan to make the original version too. Both turned out great, but things got a bit sticky when I swapped between the original recipe and the AI version in the app. You can’t save your new recipe separately, which caused confusion when I was cooking the original. Instead of scrolling back through the recipe with sticky fingers, I asked the assistant how much butter I needed (using voice typing), and it gave me the modified recipe’s 6 tablespoons rather than the original’s three-quarter cup, without telling me which version it was using. You can delete your version, which I would advise doing to avoid issues like this — 6 tablespoons of butter would have made for a dry cake.
If you already read all the comments before starting a New York Times recipe, then you will probably find this tool offers a similar experience in a more streamlined way. For everyday problems — wrong cut of chicken, no cilantro, or no Bundt cake pan — it was genuinely helpful and saved me a trip to the store. It was also smart enough to know when to say no to minced beef in a trifle.
As with every AI tool I’ve used in the kitchen, the NYT Cooking AI assistant works well as a sous chef, but it’s not the head chef — I still felt the need to double-check amounts and cooking times and cross-reference them with the original to make sure it hadn’t done something outlandish.
If you’re a purist, you can just ignore the assistant. While you can’t turn off the chat box, it’s easy to scroll past. I do think it will make me use the app more often, since I no longer need to worry as much when I don’t have makrut lime leaves on hand or the right cut of meat. For me, standing in front of the fridge with chicken tenders and no plan, it’s been a useful addition to my nightly cooking routine.
Photos by Jennifer Pattison Tuohy / The Verge

The US let Nvidia sell chips to an empty office in China
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:43:45 UTC
00:43:45 KST

Concerns are being raised that neither the Commerce Department nor Nvidia are doing their due diligence when it comes to vetting Chinese firms cleared to handle the company’s H200 AI chips. According to The New York Times, New Matrix is on the list of approved firms, yet its offices are empty, “a hollow gray shell with wires dangling from the ceiling where lights should be.” In fact, the paper could find little evidence that the company exists in any meaningful way:
The US let Nvidia sell chips to an empty office in China
The government is approving sales of Nvidia’s AI chips to Chinese firms with ties to blacklisted companies.
Only scant information can be found online about New Matrix, which was created 17 months ago. At the building that New Matrix lists as its address, a company directory does not include the firm. At the nearby property management agency for the industrial park, a receptionist and a manager both said they had no record of the company.
What little information can be found about New Matrix is concerning. Its owners have ties to a Chinese private equity firm that has been blacklisted by the US government since 2024. And according to the Times, this is not a rare lapse in oversight, but a troubling pattern of companies connected to entities with national-security-related trade restrictions being approved to purchase Nvidia chips.
Nvidia had been restricted from selling chips to China during the Biden presidency. But the Trump administration relaxed those limits after Nvidia agreed to give the government a 15-percent cut of all sales. The company’s CEO, Jensen Huang, also launched an intense lobbying campaign and was apparently targeted by China’s Ministry of State Security in a spying plot.

I Compared Marie Callender’s Legendary Frozen Chicken Pot Pie Against This Fan-Favorite, and Found the Clear Winner
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:30:00 UTC
00:30:00 KST

I Compared Marie Callender’s Legendary Frozen Chicken Pot Pie Against This Fan-Favorite, and Found the Clear Winner
There are few things more comforting to me during the cold months (which are a little longer here in New England) than chicken soup and, by extension, chicken pot pie. The flaky crust, the rich golden gravy with chunks of tender chicken, and nourishing peas and carrots — it’s a beautiful thing.
I love homemade chicken pot pie, but it can be a lot of work. I’ve probably only made a dozen or so of them in my adulthood. More often than not, I go a semi-homemade route with pre-made pie dough or, better yet, frozen puff pastry laid over the top of a casserole full of thick chicken stew. Although a homemade one is very satisfying (both in labor and taste), there’s something so nice about the convenience of a pre-made chicken pot pie.
Fortunately there are a lot of options for decent ready-to-eat pot pies, but which one is the very best? These days, when our dollars are stretched ever thinner at the grocery store, no one wants to waste money on a soggy crust or lackluster filling. So I tried two legendary options to find the very best frozen chicken pot pie.
Meet the Contenders
Not to get all Old Man Yells at Cloud on you, but there are, frankly, too many options when it comes to pot pies. There are numerous brands and then, among those brands, even more varieties like size, flavors, and pot pies even made for certain dietary restrictions (like gluten-free and vegetarian). For the purposes of this taste test, I decided to keep it simple and stick to single-serving pie from popular brands.
This is the pot pie of my youth, a classic, and if you’re very into the crust over the filling, probably the pot pie for you. After baking in the oven for 50 minutes (at 400°F), the crust browned and the thick, salty gravy covered tender bits of chicken, peas, and carrots. I found the gravy for the filling to be a bit runny as it cools, but overall it’s perfectly pleasant.
I had never tried this brand until this taste test, but I know it’s a favorite among some of my colleagues. I can see why. The sauce is very flavorful with what tastes like a decent amount of black pepper — not overpowering, but enough to know that it’s there. The chicken is very tender, and the peas, carrots, and potatoes are lovely. 
The sauce was also a bit thinner, which I appreciated. The crust is really where it’s at on this one, as it seems to be puff pastry and only a single layer of it on top. I think the addition of potatoes makes up for the lack of a bottom crust.
So, Which One Is the Best Frozen Chicken Pot Pie?
Blake’s chicken pot pie really knocked it out of the park (which, in hindsight, makes sense: Mara, director of groceries and shopping at The Kitchn, waxed poetic about it before). The crust was great — buttery and flaky — and there was exactly enough of it. 
I think Marie Callender’s pot pie, while the crust is tasty, is a bit imbalanced: too much crust, not enough filling. Again, that might be a plus for some folks. But even with my ratio preference with regard to filling and crust in mind, the filling just tasted a lot better in the Blake’s chicken pot pie. 
Consider me a convert. All hail Blake’s; long live the Blake’s.
Buy: Blake’s Chicken Pot Pie, $4.96 for 8 ounces at Walmart
What’s your favorite frozen chicken pot pie? Tell us about it in the comments below.

Show HN: We compressed a 31B video+audio diffusion model to 14B, 6 steps, INT8
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:16:01 UTC
00:16:01 KST

HyperFlow H3 Flash is Video Rebirth's compressed, few-step version of MiniMax-H3. It generates video and synchronized stereo audio together.
Showcase · Weights (Hugging Face) · ComfyUI nodes · GGUF setup guide · 8-step LoRA for full-size MiniMax-H3
- Output: 5.2–15 s of video at 24 fps (1344×768 by default) with a 32 kHz stereo soundtrack.
- Sampling: 4, 6 (default), or 8 steps, without classifier-free guidance (CFG).
- Speed (5.2 s clip, 6 steps): 8.7 s on 4× B200, 36 s on 1× H200, 92 s on 1× RTX 5090.
- Real time on 4× Blackwell GPUs: NVIDIA SOL-H3 runs HyperFlow H3 Flash in real time on 4× B-series GPUs. SOL-H3 is our inference-acceleration partner and supports this model from day 0.
- Model size: 13.7B (fl2va) or 13.9B (ref2va) parameters, less than half of the ~31B MiniMax-H3 transformer.
- Minimum footprint: Q4_K_M is 9.1 GB (Q8_0 is 14.9 GB). It runs on 24 GB and 32 GB GPUs in PCs with 16 GB of system RAM. 16 GB GPUs are not measured yet.
Decision rule: Use GGUF on PCs with 64 GB of system RAM or less. Use INT8 convrot on data-center GPUs, or on 24 GB+ RTX GPUs with at least 69 GiB of system RAM.
All weights are in VideoRebirthAI/hyperflow-h3-flash.
Full setup guide: integrations/README.md. Quick start:
- ComfyUI (Video-Rebirth/ComfyUI-HyperFlow-Flash):
  - Clone ComfyUI-GGUF and ComfyUI-HyperFlow-Flash into ComfyUI/custom_nodes/.
  - Download the 15.7 GB qwen3vl_32b_minimax_h3_nvfp4_awq text encoder.
  - On 16 GB or 32 GB PCs, start ComfyUI with python main.py --fast-disk.
  - Open Workflow > Browse Templates > ComfyUI-HyperFlow-Flash and select hyperflow_t2v, hyperflow_i2v, hyperflow_flf2v, or hyperflow_ref2v.
- Unsloth Studio (setup guide):
  - Point SD_CLI_PATH at the patched sd-cli binary and run unsloth studio.
  - Load VideoRebirthAI/hyperflow-h3-flash (fl2va_gguf/ or ref2va_gguf/).
  - Studio downloads the companion text encoder and VAEs automatically. It runs the built-in 6-step schedule at CFG = 1.
- stable-diffusion.cpp (integrations/stable-diffusion.cpp/):
Apply hyperflow-two-time.patch to upstream commit 3be2378. The patch adds the two-time (t, r) embedder, low-rank linear layers, the default 6-step schedule from GGUF metadata, and 32-bit matmul token splitting for long clips.
pip install git+https://github.com/Video-Rebirth/hyperflow-h3-flash
pip install "hyperflow-h3-flash[hopper] @ git+https://github.com/Video-Rebirth/hyperflow-h3-flash"  # H100 / H200: FlashAttention-3
pip install "hyperflow-h3-flash[fp8] @ git+https://github.com/Video-Rebirth/hyperflow-h3-flash"     # --text-encoder-precision fp8
- Requirements: Python 3.10+, PyTorch 2.6+ with CUDA and Triton (pip install triton-windows on native Windows), an Ampere or newer NVIDIA GPU, and diffusers >= 0.40. Multi-GPU (--gpus > 1) requires Linux or WSL2 (raise memory= in %UserProfile%\.wslconfig on WSL2).
- First-run download: ~90 GB, or ~68 GB with --text-encoder-precision fp8. This includes the MiniMax-H3 text encoder, VAEs, and the 16 GB HyperFlow INT8 transformer for the selected task. The 62 GB base transformer is never downloaded.
- First-run compilation: Triton INT8 kernels compile on first use (~1 min).
Run hyperflow-h3-flash or python -m hyperflow_h3_flash:
# Text to video + audio (t2va)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "A chef slides a steaming bowl of ramen across a wooden counter; rain taps on the window." \
    --output ramen.mp4
# First keyframe, or first + last keyframe (fl2va)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --image first.png --output clip.mp4
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --image first.png --last-image last.png --output clip.mp4
# Reference images, videos (with soundtrack), and audio clips in prompt order (ref2va)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "<Picture 1> walks through the scene of <Picture 2> ..." \
    --reference person.png --reference scene.png --output clip.mp4
# 15 s clip, context-parallel across 4 GPUs (or --gpus auto for up to 4 visible GPUs)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --num-frames 360 --gpus 4
# 4 steps (speed), 6 steps (default), or 8 steps (quality)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --steps 4
examples/benchmark.py times every pipeline stage and per-GPU peak memory under torchrun.
import torch
from diffusers.utils.export_utils import encode_video
from hyperflow_h3_flash import load_pipeline
pipe = load_pipeline("t2va")  # "fl2va": pass image=... (and last_image=...); "ref2va": pass references=[...]
out = pipe(
    prompt="A chef slides a steaming bowl of ramen across a wooden counter.",
    num_frames=124,
    num_inference_steps=6,  # 4, 6, or 8
    generator=torch.Generator().manual_seed(0),
    output=["videos", "audio", "sampling_rate"],
)
encode_video(
    out["videos"][0],
    fps=24,
    output_path="ramen.mp4",
    audio=out["audio"][0],
    audio_sample_rate=out["sampling_rate"],
)
- Multi-GPU under torchrun: Call torch.distributed.init_process_group("nccl") and pass device=f"cuda:{local_rank}" to load_pipeline. The pipeline shards execution across ranks and returns the full output on every rank.
- Standalone transformer (AutoModel): Each INT8 Hub folder ships self-contained model code. Load it without installing this package: from diffusers import AutoModel, then AutoModel.from_pretrained("VideoRebirthAI/hyperflow-h3-flash", subfolder="fl2va_int8_convrot", trust_remote_code=True, torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16).
--offload sets GPU memory placement. auto (default) selects a mode from per-GPU VRAM:
- stream is only ~2% slower than none on the same GPU.
- model and stream keep offloaded weights in one pinned host-memory allocation.
- The loader drops unused Qwen3-VL layers above the conditioning tap. This saves ~14 GB. --text-encoder-precision fp8 saves another ~22 GiB.
- In containers, read the memory limit from /sys/fs/cgroup/memory.max, not from free.
All runs are end-to-end at 1344×768 and 6 steps: text encoding, transformer, and video and audio VAE decode. Each a / b cell is total time for a 5.2 s / 15 s clip (124 / 362 frames).
- GGUF setup: ComfyUI, Q4_K_M, and the 15.7 GB nvfp4 text encoder. 32 GB and 16 GB PCs use --fast-disk with models on NVMe (Host RAM). Peak RSS is 34–37 GiB on ≥ 64 GB PCs. Anonymous RSS is ~8–16 GiB on 32 GB PCs and ~8 GiB on 16 GB PCs.
- INT8 setup: hyperflow-h3-flash --offload stream. It needs ≥ 91 GiB of system RAM with the bf16 text encoder, or ~69 GiB with fp8. The 15 s RTX 5090 run used fp8.
- The INT8 transformer is 10–20% faster. GGUF needs less than half the system RAM. The transformer takes over 80% of the time and is compute bound. ComfyUI dequantizes Q4_K_M weights at run time.
- Use --fast-disk on 16 GB and 32 GB PCs. It streams unresident weights from the OS file page cache. Without it, ComfyUI-GGUF copies the 9 GB transformer into pinned RAM when VRAM overflows. 16 GB PCs then run out of RAM on 15 s clips. On 32 GB PCs, the RTX 3090 and 5090 become 13% and 21% slower (text encoding takes 27–31 s instead of 7–9 s).
- 32 GB of VRAM holds both models. On the RTX 5090, the text encoder and transformer stay in VRAM together (0.8 s text encoding). On 24 GB GPUs, ComfyUI swaps them between stages (10–16 s text encoding).
- Keyframe tasks (i2v, flf2v): See GGUF guide: Performance.
- References: Two 768p stills generated with t2va (a person and a street market). Mixed is one still, one 5.2 s reference video with its soundtrack, and one audio clip.
- INT8 setup: 100–125 GB of system RAM (80–88 GiB process RSS). ¹ On 24 GB GPUs, 15 s mixed runs need --block-chunk 16384. Without it, they run out of VRAM.
- GGUF setup: ComfyUI on ≥ 64 GB RAM, with hyperflow_ref2v_api.json and the nvfp4 text encoder. ref_image_size match (default) downscales each reference to the output's pixel area and keeps its aspect ratio. max downscales only above a 2048 short edge. The max runs used the stills upscaled to 3584×2048, the size the INT8 pipeline uses. ² max at 15 s needs python main.py --disable-cuda-malloc. The default cudaMallocAsync allocator fails with CUDA error: invalid argument.
- Each reference adds tokens to every step. At a 2048 short edge, one image adds ~14,300 tokens (about seven 768p latent frames). The clip itself is 37,710 tokens at 5.2 s and 109,062 at 15 s. Attention cost grows with the square of the token count. So at 5.2 s, the INT8 transformer takes 2.6–2.9× its t2va time with two images and 5.2–5.8× with the mixed set.
- GGUF match is the fastest image-reference path. It adds 24–36% to t2v at 5.2 s and 7–15% at 15 s. At the same reference size (max), GGUF is 11–25% slower than INT8 at 5.2 s and 8–9% slower at 15 s.
- --fast-disk also works for ref2v. 32 GB and 16 GB PCs ran 5.2 s and 15 s clips on all three GPUs, within 4% of ≥ 64 GB RAM at 15 s. See GGUF guide: Host RAM.
- Real-time inference: The table measures this package. For real time on 4× B-series GPUs, use NVIDIA SOL-H3.
- INT8 GEMM backend: The 4× H200 row uses the default Triton kernel (12.6 s). int8_gemm="sgl" (CUTLASS INT8 via sgl-kernel) measured 12.3 s.
- 4-GPU stage times (5.2 s): Text encoder 0.11–0.16 s. 6-step transformer 7.8–7.9 s on 4× H200 and 5.0 s on 4× B200. Video VAE decode 3.3–4.0 s.
- 1088p (1920×1088, 362 frames): 743 s on 1× H200 (106 GiB VRAM). 220 s on 4× H200 (68 GiB VRAM/GPU).
HyperFlow H3 Flash changes the MiniMax-H3 transformer in three ways:
- Structural pruning and low-rank factorization:
- Rotated W8A8 INT8 quantization (convrot): 180 linear layers are quantized to W8A8 INT8 behind a block-diagonal Hadamard rotation.
- Two-time (t, r) conditioning: Every token receives the current noise level t and the step endpoint r. This enables 4-to-8-step sampling without CFG. Conditioning tokens stay at their anchor noise level (r = t).
- 
Sequence and VAE parallelism (--gpus N): Ulysses context parallelism splits the packed transformer sequence across$N$ GPUs. Ranks encode keyframes and references and decode spatial video VAE tiles in parallel. Then they all-gather and blend the tiles to match single-GPU output bitwise.
- 
Text encoder placement: With --offload none and the bf16 text encoder, FSDP2 shards the 50 GiB text encoder across GPUs. This cuts 4-GPU peak VRAM from 91 GiB to 59 GiB/GPU. When offloading or using fp8, rank 0 runs the text encoder and broadcasts the embeddings (~2 MB).
- INT8 GEMM dispatch:
- Reproducibility: The same seed, GPU architecture, GPU count, and attention backend produce bitwise-identical clips. text_encoder_fsdp and vae_parallel preserve bitwise output.
make install   # create .venv and install editable package with dev tools
make test      # run unit tests on tiny models (CUDA tests skip without a GPU)
make lint      # ruff check + format check.
├── src/hyperflow_h3_flash/        # Python package (INT8 runtime)
│   ├── cli.py                     # `hyperflow-h3-flash` CLI entry point
│   ├── __main__.py                # `python -m hyperflow_h3_flash` entry point
│   ├── hostmem.py                 # Host RAM estimate before loading
│   ├── pipeline.py                # `load_pipeline`: loads models, trims unused text-encoder layers, configures offload/multi-GPU
│   ├── blocks.py                  # Modular Pipeline blocks swapped into the official MiniMax-H3 workflows
│   ├── transformer.py             # `HyperFlowH3FlashTransformer3DModel`: dropped blocks, low-rank linears, two-time (t, r) conditioning
│   ├── int8.py                    # Hadamard-rotated W8A8 linear layers (Triton, cuBLASLt, CUTLASS, and B300 BF16 kernels)
│   ├── schedule.py                # 4-, 6-, and 8-step sigma grids and per-token (t, r) pairs
│   ├── offload.py                 # Single-GPU memory placement (`none`, `model`, `stream`)
│   └── distributed.py             # Multi-GPU execution (torchrun launch, Ulysses attention, FSDP2 / rank-0 text encoder, parallel VAE)
├── integrations/                  # GGUF runtimes for consumer GPUs
│   ├── README.md                  # Step-by-step setup guide for ComfyUI, Unsloth Studio, and stable-diffusion.cpp
│   ├── ComfyUI-HyperFlow-Flash/   # Custom nodes (`loader_nodes.py`, `model_patch.py`, `gguf_io.py`; GPL-3.0)
│   │   └── example_workflows/     # Four UI workflows (`hyperflow_t2v`, `hyperflow_i2v`, `hyperflow_flf2v`, `hyperflow_ref2v`) and API versions in `api/`
│   └── stable-diffusion.cpp/      # `hyperflow-two-time.patch` for upstream commit `3be2378` (MIT)
├── examples/                      # `benchmark.py` (per-stage timings and peak memory)
├── tests/                         # Unit tests on tiny models (CUDA tests skip without a GPU)

├── pyproject.toml                 # Package metadata and optional extras (`hopper`, `sgl`, `fp8`, `dev`)

Show HN: We compressed a 31B video+audio diffusion model to 14B, 6 steps, INT8
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:16:01 UTC
00:16:01 KST

HyperFlow H3 Flash is Video Rebirth's compressed, few-step version of MiniMax-H3. It generates video and synchronized stereo audio together.
Showcase · Weights (Hugging Face) · ComfyUI nodes · GGUF setup guide · 8-step LoRA for full-size MiniMax-H3
- Output: 5.2–15 s of video at 24 fps (1344×768 by default) with a 32 kHz stereo soundtrack.
- Sampling: 4, 6 (default), or 8 steps, without classifier-free guidance (CFG).
- Speed (5.2 s clip, 6 steps): 8.7 s on 4× B200, 36 s on 1× H200, 92 s on 1× RTX 5090.
- Real time on 4× Blackwell GPUs: NVIDIA SOL-H3 runs HyperFlow H3 Flash in real time on 4× B-series GPUs. SOL-H3 is our inference-acceleration partner and supports this model from day 0.
- Model size: 13.7B (fl2va) or 13.9B (ref2va) parameters, less than half of the ~31B MiniMax-H3 transformer.
- Minimum footprint: Q4_K_M is 9.1 GB (Q8_0 is 14.9 GB). It runs on 24 GB and 32 GB GPUs in PCs with 16 GB of system RAM. 16 GB GPUs are not measured yet.
Decision rule: Use GGUF on PCs with 64 GB of system RAM or less. Use INT8 convrot on data-center GPUs, or on 24 GB+ RTX GPUs with at least 69 GiB of system RAM.
All weights are in VideoRebirthAI/hyperflow-h3-flash.
Full setup guide: integrations/README.md. Quick start:
- ComfyUI (Video-Rebirth/ComfyUI-HyperFlow-Flash):
  - Clone ComfyUI-GGUF and ComfyUI-HyperFlow-Flash into ComfyUI/custom_nodes/.
  - Download the 15.7 GB qwen3vl_32b_minimax_h3_nvfp4_awq text encoder.
  - On 16 GB or 32 GB PCs, start ComfyUI with python main.py --fast-disk.
  - Open Workflow > Browse Templates > ComfyUI-HyperFlow-Flash and select hyperflow_t2v, hyperflow_i2v, hyperflow_flf2v, or hyperflow_ref2v.
- Unsloth Studio (setup guide):
  - Point SD_CLI_PATH at the patched sd-cli binary and run unsloth studio.
  - Load VideoRebirthAI/hyperflow-h3-flash (fl2va_gguf/ or ref2va_gguf/).
  - Studio downloads the companion text encoder and VAEs automatically. It runs the built-in 6-step schedule at CFG = 1.
- stable-diffusion.cpp (integrations/stable-diffusion.cpp/):
Apply hyperflow-two-time.patch to upstream commit 3be2378. The patch adds the two-time (t, r) embedder, low-rank linear layers, the default 6-step schedule from GGUF metadata, and 32-bit matmul token splitting for long clips.
pip install git+https://github.com/Video-Rebirth/hyperflow-h3-flash
pip install "hyperflow-h3-flash[hopper] @ git+https://github.com/Video-Rebirth/hyperflow-h3-flash"  # H100 / H200: FlashAttention-3
pip install "hyperflow-h3-flash[fp8] @ git+https://github.com/Video-Rebirth/hyperflow-h3-flash"     # --text-encoder-precision fp8
- Requirements: Python 3.10+, PyTorch 2.6+ with CUDA and Triton (pip install triton-windows on native Windows), an Ampere or newer NVIDIA GPU, and diffusers >= 0.40. Multi-GPU (--gpus > 1) requires Linux or WSL2 (raise memory= in %UserProfile%\.wslconfig on WSL2).
- First-run download: ~90 GB, or ~68 GB with --text-encoder-precision fp8. This includes the MiniMax-H3 text encoder, VAEs, and the 16 GB HyperFlow INT8 transformer for the selected task. The 62 GB base transformer is never downloaded.
- First-run compilation: Triton INT8 kernels compile on first use (~1 min).
Run hyperflow-h3-flash or python -m hyperflow_h3_flash:
# Text to video + audio (t2va)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "A chef slides a steaming bowl of ramen across a wooden counter; rain taps on the window." \
    --output ramen.mp4
# First keyframe, or first + last keyframe (fl2va)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --image first.png --output clip.mp4
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --image first.png --last-image last.png --output clip.mp4
# Reference images, videos (with soundtrack), and audio clips in prompt order (ref2va)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "<Picture 1> walks through the scene of <Picture 2> ..." \
    --reference person.png --reference scene.png --output clip.mp4
# 15 s clip, context-parallel across 4 GPUs (or --gpus auto for up to 4 visible GPUs)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --num-frames 360 --gpus 4
# 4 steps (speed), 6 steps (default), or 8 steps (quality)
hyperflow-h3-flash --prompt "..." --steps 4

Ask HN: How did you convince family and friends to join your XMPP/Matrix server?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:14:40 UTC
00:14:40 KST

I love open-source software and open protocols, but my family and friends primarily communicate through SMS for exactly the reasons in https://xkcd.com/2365/
Whenever XMPP or Matrix threads pop up on HN, several commenters casually mention their "friends and family XMPP (or Matrix) server."
How did you convince friends and family to move away from SMS/WhatsApp and replace it with a niche messaging app?
My family and friends aren't hardcore nerdy, but they're more tech-savvy than average. Their needs in messaging are mainly pragmatic: they want ease of use, reliability, and fun features. They don't care that much about privacy, decentralization, or extensibility (though I care about those things a lot).
I've been experimenting with self-hosting XMPP and Matrix servers and clients in the past week, and the UX is even worse than I expected. It seems like all the clients either imitate Slack, imitate Discord, or imitate hot garbage. I haven't seen any that try to be a simple, friendly replacement for SMS. I'm a software developer who uses computers all day, and I find the UX for most of the clients incredibly hard to use.
The only angle I could see to pitch XMPP/Matrix to people for non-philosophical reasons are that there's a more consistent experience across iOS/Android. The biggest shortcoming I think is visible to regular users is that there's feature degradation and missing information when iOS users have SMS conversations with Android users, especially in group chats.
For people who have successfully won family and friends to XMPP or Matrix, what was your pitch? What do you recommend others do to make XMPP or Matrix an attractive experience for a regular person?

====================================================================================================

It seems like all the clients either imitate Slack, imitate Discord, or imitate hot garbage. I haven't seen any that try to be a simple, friendly replacement for SMS. I'm a software developer who uses computers all day, and I find the UX for most of the clients incredibly hard to use.
The only angle I could see to pitch XMPP/Matrix to people for non-philosophical reasons are that there's a more consistent experience across iOS/Android. The biggest shortcoming I think is visible to regular users is that there's feature degradation and missing information when iOS users have SMS conversations with Android users, especially in group chats.
For people who have successfully won family and friends to XMPP or Matrix, what was your pitch? What do you recommend others do to make XMPP or Matrix an attractive experience for a regular person?

French Country Pot Roast
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:00:00 UTC
00:00:00 KST

French Country Pot Roast
Tender beef braises low and slow in a white wine sauce with potatoes, carrots, and pearl onions.
Serves4 to 6
Prep30 minutes
Cook3 hours 30 minutes to 4 hours
Why You’ll Love My French Country Pot Roast
- It’s a cozy pot roast with a French twist. White wine gives the sauce a brighter, lighter flavor than red wine versions, and smoky bacon, Dijon, and fresh herbs add other layers.
- Dinner cooks in just one pot. The beef, mushrooms, potatoes, carrots, and pearl onions all braise together in the same Dutch oven, so you get a full meal with minimal cleanup.
This pot roast tastes like you spent all afternoon in the kitchen, but you only need about 30 minutes of prep before the oven takes over and fills your house with the most amazing aroma. Inspired by daube Provençale (the classic slow-braised French beef stew that can be made with either red or white wine), I turned the white wine version into this hearty pot roast. I’m a big fan of pot roast made with red wine, like my French onion pot roast, so using white wine felt like a great twist.
The result is a French-inspired pot roast with a brighter, more delicate flavor, built on smoky bacon, Dijon mustard, and fresh herbs. The beef braises low and slow until it’s tender enough to shred, with mushrooms, potatoes, carrots, and pearl onions cooking in the same pot. I like to serve it in shallow bowls with plenty of crusty bread for soaking up the sauce, and a simple green salad on the side to keep things fresh.
Straight from Our Recipe Tester
“I couldn’t help myself but drag every bite of potato, beef, and carrot through more broth. I just couldn’t get enough of it. The beef became so tender in the oven that it just fell apart when shredding. Now that it’s pot roast weather, I could see this on my monthly rotation.” —Celia, October 2026
Why You Should Trust Me
I graduated from Le Cordon Bleu in Chicago, and have spent more than 10 years working in test kitchens. A big part of my job is testing and developing recipes for the team, so I’m always thinking about how recipes will actually work in a home kitchen. I pay close attention to the little details, so every recipe is approachable, reliable, and worth making again.
Key Ingredients in French Country Pot Roast
- Thick-cut bacon: Bacon adds smoky flavor to the sauce, and its rendered fat is perfect for searing the roast.
- Beef chuck roast: This well-marbled cut turns meltingly tender after a long braise in the oven, and it’s easy to shred right in the pot.
- Dry white wine: It forms the base of the sauce, adding a bright, slightly tangy flavor that sets this pot roast apart from red wine versions.
- Vegetables: Cremini mushrooms, baby potatoes, carrots, and pearl onions simmer in the sauce until tender, soaking up all that flavor and making this a complete one-pot meal.
How to Make French Country Pot Roast
- Cook the bacon and sear the roast. Cook the bacon until crisp and set it aside, then season the roast and sear it in the same pot until well-browned on both sides.
- Build the sauce. Sauté the onion and mushrooms, scraping up the browned bits, then stir in the Dijon, garlic, and flour. Stir in the broth, wine, bacon, thyme, and rosemary.
- Braise the roast. Return the roast to the pot, bring the liquid to a simmer, cover, and transfer to the oven to braise until the beef is nearly tender.
- Add the vegetables and finish. Flip the roast; scatter the potatoes, carrots, and pearl onions around it; and continue cooking uncovered until everything is tender. Discard the herb stems, shred the roast right in the pot, season to taste, and garnish with parsley if you like.
Helpful Swaps
- Mushrooms: White button mushrooms work just as well as cremini, or you can use a mix of both.
- Potatoes: You can use larger gold or red potatoes instead of baby potatoes. Just cut them into 2-inch chunks first so they cook evenly.
- Pearl onions: If you can’t find frozen pearl onions, you can use trimmed and peeled fresh pearl onions. For the easiest and fastest way to peel them, blanch the pearl onions in boiling water first, then shock them in cold water.
Make-Ahead and Storage Tips
- Make ahead: The pot roast can be made up to 1 day ahead. Let cool completely, then cover and refrigerate. Reheat, covered, over medium heat on the stovetop or in a 325ºF oven until warmed through.
- Storage: Leftovers can be refrigerated in an airtight container for up to 4 days, or frozen for up to 3 months.
What to Serve with French Country Pot Roast
French Country Pot Roast Recipe
Tender beef braises low and slow in a white wine sauce with potatoes, carrots, and pearl onions.
Prep time 30 minutes
Cook time 3 hours 30 minutes to 4 hours
Serves 4 to 6
Nutritional Info
Ingredients
- 4 ounces thick-cut bacon (3 to 4 slices), cut crosswise into 1-inch pieces
- 1 (3-pound) boneless beef chuck roast, trimmed of surface fat
- 2 teaspoons kosher salt, plus more as needed
- 3/4 teaspoon freshly ground black pepper, divided, plus more as needed
- 1 medium yellow onion, diced (1 1/2 cups)
- 8 ounces cremini mushrooms
- 2 tablespoons Dijon mustard
- 4 cloves garlic, minced (1 tablespoon)
- 3 1/2 tablespoons all-purpose flour
- 1 1/2 cups low-sodium beef broth
- 1 (750-milliliter) bottle dry white wine
- 3 sprigs fresh thyme
- 2 sprigs fresh rosemary
- 3 medium carrots
- 1 pound baby yellow, gold, or red potatoes (1 1/2 to 2 inches wide)
- 1 cup frozen pearl onions
- Chopped fresh parsley leaves, for garnish (optional)
Instructions
- Arrange a rack in the lower third of the oven and heat the oven to 350℉.
- Cook 4 ounces chopped thick-cut bacon in a 5-quart or larger Dutch oven or heavy-bottomed oven-safe pot with a tight-fitting lid over medium heat, stirring occasionally, until the bacon is browned and crisp, 8 to 10 minutes.
- Meanwhile, pat 1 (about 3-pound) boneless beef chuck roast dry with paper towels. Season all over with 2 teaspoons kosher salt and 1/2 teaspoon of the black pepper.
- When the bacon is ready, transfer it to a large plate with a slotted spoon. Add the roast to the pot and sear until well-browned on two sides, 5 to 6 minutes per side. Transfer to the plate with the bacon.
- Add 1 diced medium yellow onion and 8 ounces whole cremini mushrooms to the pot. Cook, stirring occasionally and scraping up the browned bits from the bottom of the pot, until the onions are softened, about 5 minutes.
- Stir in 2 tablespoons Dijon mustard, 4 minced garlic cloves, and the remaining 1/4 teaspoon black pepper. Sprinkle with 3 1/2 tablespoons all-purpose flour. Stir to coat and cook for 1 minute to cook the floury taste out.
- Stir in 1 1/2 cups low-sodium beef broth and scrape up any browned bits from the bottom of the pot. Stir in the bacon, 1 (750-ml) bottle dry white wine, 3 fresh thyme sprigs, and 2 fresh rosemary sprigs. Return the roast and any accumulated juices to the pot. Bring to a simmer.
- Cover and transfer the pot to the oven. Cook for 2 hours. Meanwhile, peel and cut 3 medium carrots crosswise into 2-inch pieces. If any pieces are wider than 1 1/2 inches, halve them lengthwise.
- Uncover and flip the roast. Scatter 1 pound baby yellow potatoes around the roast, making sure they are submerged in the liquid. Scatter the carrots and 1 cup frozen pearl onions around the roast, making sure they are mostly submerged. Return to the oven and cook uncovered until the roast is very tender and a knife slides easily into the center of the roast, 1 to 1 1/2 hours more.
- Taste the sauce and season with kosher salt as needed. Remove and discard the thyme and rosemary stems. Shred the roast right in the pot into bite-size pieces with tongs. Garnish with chopped fresh parsley leaves if desired.
Recipe Notes
Make ahead: The pot roast can be made up to 1 day ahead. Let cool completely, then cover and refrigerate. Reheat covered over medium heat on the stovetop or in a 325ºF oven until warmed through.
Storage: Leftovers can be refrigerated in an airtight container for up to 4 days, or frozen for up to 3 months.

Discogs’ Kevin Lewandowski can’t live without his AeroPress
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
14:45:08 UTC
23:45:08 KST

Twenty-five years ago, Kevin Lewandowski founded Discogs, arguably the destination online for obsessive vinyl collectors and music nerds of all stripes. It is a marketplace for buying and selling records, obviously, but it’s also become so much more. It’s a shockingly comprehensive database that covers not just albums and singles, but all the various versions and pressings. It’s a really fun rabbit hole to disappear down when you’re trying to figure out what obscure pressing of Ministry’s The Mind is a Terrible Thing to Taste or Psalm 69 you had in high school, because you definitely blasted the cover of “Lay Lady Lay” all the time and 100 percent never owned Filth Pig.
Discogs’ Kevin Lewandowski can’t live without his AeroPress
The founder of Discogs talks coffee, Rez, and (of course) records.
Discogs’ Kevin Lewandowski can’t live without his AeroPress
The founder of Discogs talks coffee, Rez, and (of course) records.
In the years since it debuted, Discogs has grown to be much more than just a site where audiophiles go to trade rare first pressings of Beach Boys records. There are forums, groups, user-created lists, and more. It’s part eBay, part Reddit, and IMDB — just specifically for music.
With more and more people turning their back on the algorithm and rediscovering physical media, now seemed like a perfect time to check in with Lewandowski.
What is your most indispensable tool?
My AeroPress. I use it multiple times a day. In fact, I just made a coffee with it [sips coffee].
How many tabs do you have open right now?
Probably around 30 on my work computer. My personal computer is worse — probably 40 or 50.
Which social media platform do you use the most?
I look at X and Instagram occasionally, but I don’t really post.
What is your favorite gadget you’ve ever owned?
It’s not really a gadget, but I like to tinker. Lately I’ve been playing around with Home Assistant software and built my own setup to control things like the garage door and outdoor lights. So I guess my favorite gadget is one I made myself.
What is one thing you wish you had created?
Probably a game like Rez, which came out around 2000. It’s a rhythm game where you’re almost controlling the music as you play, about as close to DJing as a video game can get. I’ve actually had an idea for my own version with a similar concept. It’s still just an idea, though.
What do you do when you need to focus?
I’ll either clear everything off my desk or change locations completely. Go to a coffee shop or sit outside. Sometimes I just need a different environment.
What’s the last piece of physical media you bought?
Records. I was at Mint Gallery in Portland last week and picked up four. I still really like going into a record store and finding something to bring home. And I also recently picked up three reissues from the Moodsville / Acoustic Sounds series.
What would the tagline for your biopic be?
The Secret Weapon.
What’s the last GIF or meme you used?
I don’t use memes very often, but one I was kind of proud of was putting a Discogs screen into a North Korean war room photo. I was advised that maybe it wasn’t one for the company chat.

Good riddance, Cybertruck!
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
14:40:06 UTC
23:40:06 KST

I’m calling it: time of death, November 6th, 1:30 AM, by tweet.
Good riddance, Cybertruck!
We’ll always have our comically oversized windshield wiper.
Elon Musk’s declaration Friday that Tesla would be “winding down” production of the Cybertruck next year to make room for the Cybercab was fascinating for a number of reasons. First, it was a humiliating way to kill a vehicle that was once hailed as the pinnacle of Tesla’s engineering — faster than a Porsche 911, more powerful than an F-150, able to leap over the Model Y as a singular representation of Musk’s Blade Runner-inspired designs for the future. “I think the Tesla Cybertruck is our best product ever,” he declared during a 2019 earnings call. (He prefaced the statement by saying, “This could be totally wrong. I could be totally out to lunch here.” It would appear so!)
The fact that Musk would just quietly put an end to the Cybertruck without any fanfare was a sign of how far it had fallen, not just in the eyes of the public, but in the eyes of its creator, the man most responsible for brute-forcing this low-emission, low-poly technological nightmare into the world.
Tesla actually thought the Cybertruck would be a huge hit, targeting production and demand of 250,000 units per year. To date, the company has only sold 70,000 units — total. Musk’s other company, SpaceX, ended up being one of the largest single purchasers of Cybertrucks to absorb unsold inventory. Musk had to become his own customer to make the Cybertruck look better on paper.
But the death of the Cybertruck is even more noteworthy given the timing of Musk’s tweet, coming just three days after his epic flop at the ballot box. Musk poured over $445 million into Republican races, with most of his money targeting his home turf of Texas — headquarters to both Tesla and SpaceX. Between funnels of $168 million to super PACs backing Republican Senate candidate Ken Paxton and nearly $35 million redirected from his own America PAC, Musk dropped more than $200 million on the Lone Star State alone.
All that money, and zero return. Democrat James Talarico crushed Paxton by 12 points to break a 38-year Democratic Senate drought, while Gina Hinojosa ousted Greg Abbott from the governor’s mansion. Texas turned blue, despite the best efforts of the world’s richest man — or perhaps because of them?
Americans are fed up: they don’t want Musk meddling in their government, and they especially don’t want his stainless-steel monument to his own vanity and utter refusal to accept human feedback.
The Cybertruck was one of the most epic failures in modern automotive history. While it succeeded as a viral PR spectacle and initially became a top-selling premium electric pickup in early 2024, it ultimately collapsed under the weight of Musk’s own hubris.
The reasons for the Cybertruck’s failures are almost too many to enumerate: too expensive, not as much range or towing power as promised, too many recalls, and, well, it looked the way it did. But when Musk outed himself as a Trump supporter, forever tying himself to Trump’s MAGA brand, the Cybertruck went with him. Electric vehicles are typically associated with a style of coastal progressivism, but the Cybertruck crossed the aisle to become a physical avatar of Trump’s ugly brand of politics.
And more so than any other car in modern history, the Cybertruck was inseparable from its creator. By the time Musk threw hundreds of millions of dollars into Trump’s campaign, launched the America PAC, and took a prominent position in Trump’s administration as the head of the disastrous DOGE project, owning a Cybertruck had become a rolling liability. People who owned a Model 3 or Model Y could credibly claim they bought it before “Elon went crazy,” as the bumper stickers would later declare — a Cybertruck owner could make no such claim.
To be sure, most people who bought Cybertrucks were not interested in distancing themselves from Musk’s brand of trollish politics. A big part of MAGA culture is owning the libs. And because liberals, car purists, and environmentalists couldn’t stand the Cybertruck — mocking everything from its sharp edges to its build quality — buying one became a way to get under their skin. Before long, people were slapping Trump wraps on them, flying flags from the truck beds, and turning them into props for conservative rallies.
But vibes shift, as they always do. Edgelords lose their edge, and voters become weary and wary of efforts to divide them. The blue tsunami was a historic rejection of Trump and Musk’s brand of right-wing grievance politics. When the spectacle wore off, the bluster stopped feeling like rebellion and started looking like a shit way to run a government. In a single election, the country said it was done.
The results on Tuesday also rendered the Cybertruck an instant relic. The election stripped away the sheen of inevitability that once insulated both the billionaire and his pet project. It’s a relief, but a peculiar one at that. Has there ever been a vehicle so desperate to look like the future, only to become an immediate symbol of the past?

19 Easy Corn Recipes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

Corn has quite the résumé for a humble grocery staple. It’s sweet and juicy straight off the cob in summer, but hand me a bag of frozen kernels, a can of corn, or a little cornmeal once the weather cools down, and I am still very much interested. I’m talking golden cornbread, creamy Thanksgiving sides, smoky street corn salads, and even a risotto that makes use of those leftover cobs (yes, really!). These 19 easy corn recipes prove you can get a LOT of mileage out of this affordable ingredient long after fresh corn season wraps up!
All recipes are rigorously tested in our Nashville Test Kitchen to ensure they are easy, affordable, and delicious.
Cornbread Recipes Worth Saving Room For
There’s always a good reason to bake a batch of cornbread. It’s sweet, tender, and so very inexpensive to make. The basic recipe is already pretty wonderful, but throw in some cheese, sweet potato, or a drizzle of spicy honey, and suddenly you’ve got a whole new reason to make another batch.
1. Homemade Cornbread
Easy Homemade Cornbread Recipe
The OG. The one everybody should have in their back pocket! Our homemade cornbread is made entirely from scratch with a handful of pantry staples, and it takes just 30 minutes from start to finish. It’s lightly sweet, beautifully tender, and honestly, I could eat a warm slice with butter any time of day. If you don’t know, now you know. 😉
2. Sweet Potato Cornbread
Sweet Potato Cornbread
Can we talk about just how good this sweet potato cornbread is?! Mashed sweet potato and sour cream make the crumb extra rich and moist, while a little cinnamon and nutmeg give it the most delicious warmth. It’s sweeter and denser than traditional cornbread, and I love serving it with a big bowl of smoky bean chili.
3. Chili Cheese Cornbread
Chili Cheese Cornbread
This one takes the shortcut route, and I’m not mad at it. Two boxes of cornbread mix get a serious flavor upgrade with chili powder, sweet corn kernels, and a generous layer of shredded cheddar. The result is a slightly spicy, cheesy chili cheese cornbread that’s ready in about 30 minutes.
4. Hot Honey Cornbread
Hot Honey Cornbread
Remember when hot honey was having a moment? Yeah, I’m still not over it. 😋 This hot honey cornbread takes sweet, buttery cornbread and gives it a little kick with a warm honey-butter drizzle and red pepper flakes. Sour cream keeps the inside deliciously soft and cake-like, and that drizzle of sweet heat makes it an excellent partner for savory dinners.
Thanksgiving Staples
Corn comes in many delicious disguises at Thanksgiving! From rich and creamy to fluffy and golden, there’s plenty to love about these classic holiday sides. Better yet, they’re made with affordable ingredients, so you can put on a delicious spread without blowing your holiday grocery budget.
5. Corn Pudding
Corn Pudding
Soft, creamy, cheesy, and just sweet enough to keep you coming back for another spoonful. This homemade corn pudding is somewhere between a savory custard and a very soft cornbread, with juicy corn kernels suspended in a rich mixture of eggs, half-and-half, sour cream, and cheddar. It’s made completely from scratch, with no boxed mixes or canned creamed corn! Frozen corn also keeps things convenient and brings the cost down to around $0.80 per serving.
6. Creamed Corn
Creamed Corn Recipe
I’m officially voting to leave the canned cream-style corn on the grocery store shelf this Thanksgiving! My homemade creamed corn is buttery and silky, with sweet corn and finished with Parmesan cheese for extra savory flavor. I make a simple roux to thicken the sauce, and frozen corn works just as well as fresh. The whole thing takes only 25 minutes, costs about $0.66 per serving, and is SO much tastier than anything from a can.
7. Cornbread Dressing
Cornbread Dressing
If you’re looking to change things up from traditional bread stuffing, give this Southern-style cornbread dressing a try. Crumbled homemade cornbread and toasted white bread soak up a flavorful mixture of broth, butter, sautéed vegetables, and herbs before baking until golden. It’s savory, moist, and slightly crumbly, with those lovely crisp bits around the edges. Better yet, one batch makes 12 generous servings, so it’s a smart choice when you’re feeding a crowd!
8. Cornbread Casserole
Cornbread Casserole
Two Thanksgiving sides, one baking dish. Now THAT is holiday cooking I can get behind! This cornbread casserole combines whole corn kernels, cream-style corn, cheddar, and boxed cornbread mix for a rich, buttery bake with a soft, creamy middle and a lightly crisp, golden top. It’s a little more substantial than corn pudding, but much softer than regular cornbread. I love how it’s an affordable way to get two favorites onto the table at once.
A Little Taste of Summer
There’s nothing quite like fresh sweet corn in the summertime, especially when the ears are piled high at the farmers market or on sale at the grocery store! That’s usually when you’ll find the best flavor and prices, so I like taking full advantage. These recipes make those fresh kernels shine, but you’ll find a few clever frozen corn options in the mix, too.
9. Cowboy Caviar
Cowboy Caviar
A bowl of cowboy caviar never lasts long once the tortilla chips come out! This colorful dip starts with inexpensive black beans and black-eyed peas, then adds crunchy vegetables and a zesty homemade lime dressing. I highly recommend stirring in some fresh or thawed frozen corn for extra sweetness and crunch. It’s a low-cost way to stretch the batch, and any leftovers are delicious spooned over tacos, salads, or rice bowls.
10. Street Corn Salad
Street Corn Salad (Esquites)
All the tangy, creamy, spicy flavors of Mexican street corn, minus the messy cob! I quickly char fresh corn kernels in a hot skillet to give my street corn salad that delicious roasted flavor, then toss them with cotija cheese, cilantro, and a creamy chili-lime dressing. It’s inspired by elote and esquites, and this salad is ALWAYS on repeat at my house during the summer.
11. Sweet Corn Fritters
Sweet Corn Fritters
Crispy golden edges, soft centers, little bursts of sweet corn, and salty feta cheese. Need I say more?! These sweet corn fritters are a delicious little appetizer, snack, or light lunch, especially with a dollop of sour cream or aioli for dipping. I make them with fresh corn when it’s in season, but frozen works too! Just make sure you thaw and dry the kernels thoroughly before frying to avoid excess oil splatter.
12. Street Corn Pasta Salad
Elote (Street Corn) Pasta Salad
My street corn salad has a pasta-loving cousin, and it’s this elote-inspired pasta salad! This easy recipe mixes tender ditalini with charred corn, poblano peppers, queso fresco, and a creamy chipotle-lime dressing. It has all those smoky, tangy, cheesy flavors I love, but with pasta to make it a little more filling. You can also prep it ahead for potlucks and cookouts. Plus, using frozen corn helps keep the cost at a very reasonable $0.69 per serving.
13. Sweet Corn Salsa
Sweet Corn Salsa
No cooking required for this one! Raw sweet corn is surprisingly juicy and crunchy, and it’s absolutely delicious tossed with ripe tomatoes, jalapeño, red onion, cilantro, and lime. This fresh sweet corn salsa takes just 15 minutes to make, and it’s fantastic with tortilla chips or piled onto tacos. If you’ve only ever eaten cooked corn, this is your sign to try something different.
14. Sweet Corn Risotto
Sweet Corn Risotto
Here’s a little kitchen thrift I can fully get behind…don’t throw away those corn cobs! For my sweet corn risotto, I use the stripped cobs to infuse the cooking broth with extra sweetness and earthy corn flavor. The tender kernels add a lovely contrast to the creamy Arborio rice, butter, and Parmesan cheese. It’s rich and comforting without needing any heavy cream, and using every last bit of the corn makes it even more satisfying to prepare.
Corn + Zucchini: A Very Good Match
Corn and zucchini come into season around the same time, which is handy when your garden (or the farmers market!) is overflowing with both. Sweet corn gives zucchini a lovely pop of flavor and texture, and together they can make everything from a hearty baked pie to a crunchy raw salad. If you’ve still got more squash to use, our zucchini recipes roundup has plenty of other ideas, too!
15. Sweet Corn and Zucchini Pie
Sweet Corn and Zucchini Pie
Who needs a pastry crust when the filling is this good?! This sweet corn and zucchini pie combines fresh corn, zucchini, yellow squash, eggs, cottage cheese, and mozzarella into a creamy bake. No pastry crust means less time and money, and one pie makes six generous servings for about $1.21 each. I’d happily put this on the table for brunch, lunch, or a light dinner.
16. Sweet Corn Salad
Summer Sweet Corn Salad
Don’t overlook the zucchini hiding in this sweet corn salad. It adds a lovely fresh crunch alongside raw sweet corn, juicy tomatoes, creamy avocado, and tender little pasta shells. Everything gets tossed in a bright lemon-Dijon vinaigrette, and the pasta makes it substantial enough for a satisfying lunch.
17. Zucchini, Corn, and Toasted Almond Salad
Zucchini, Corn, and Toasted Almond Salad
A textural wonderland, truly! I leave both the zucchini AND sweet corn raw in my zucchini, corn, and toasted almond salad, which keeps everything wonderfully fresh and crisp. Toasted almonds add a buttery crunch, ribbons of Parmesan bring a little saltiness, and a homemade lemon-mint dressing ties it all together. There’s no need to heat the oven, it’s ready in 25 minutes, and each serving costs just $0.99!
Corn on the Cob
Sometimes, a little butter and salt are all sweet corn really needs. You can let the grill do its thing for that lovely summer flavor or take the quick and easy route with the microwave. Either method gives you juicy, tender kernels without much fuss, so pick whichever suits your day.
18. Grilled Corn on the Cob
Grilled Corn on the Cob
I’ve loved corn on the cob since I was a kid, and grilling it is still one of my favorite ways to enjoy it. For my grilled corn on the cob, I soak the ears and cook them right in their husks, letting the corn steam inside while the outside gets beautifully charred. There’s no need to buy aluminum foil, so that’s one less thing to spend money on or throw away! A grill pan also works when cooking outdoors isn’t an option.
19. Microwave Corn on the Cob
Microwave Corn On The Cob
Four minutes per ear, give or take your microwave’s wattage. That’s all it takes to make perfectly juicy microwave corn on the cob! The corn steams right in its husk, so you don’t need to boil a big pot of water. Add a little butter and salt, and you’ve got one of the easiest corn side dishes imaginable.

The $8 Linux Computer (2022)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
14:00:20 UTC
23:00:20 KST

Yes, you read that right... for $8 you can run linux on a RISC-V computer. And not just a stripped down kernel without an MMU. No, the Ox64 comes with capable RISC-V cores. Heck, and not just one core, but three cores (all different.) Exciting times!
Caveat Emptor! At the time of writing, ethernet, wifi and SD-Card are not working. It's good to realize this before jumping into this.
It looks like a microcontroller, but behaves like a UNIX machine. Sweet, or what? Getting it up and running, though, is far from trivial. There are so many hurdles to take!
Hurdle 1: You cannot program it using USB, like you would an Arduino, ESP32, Stamp-C3 or rPi Pico. Nope... this needs to be programmed via the UART TX/RX pins. In the picture above, you can see them hooked up to GPIO14 / GPIO15 and GND.
Hudle 2: The flashing tool Dev Cube will crash on launch when using Ubuntu on Wayland: you have to switch to Xorg instead.
$ ./BLDevCube-ubuntu 
Warning: Ignoring XDG_SESSION_TYPE=wayland on Gnome. Use QT_QPA_PLATFORM=wayland to run on Wayland anyway.
/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/gio/modules/libdconfsettings.so: undefined symbol: g_log_structured_standard
Failed to load module: /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/gio/modules/libdconfsettings.so
GLib-GIO-Message: Using the 'memory' GSettings backend.  Your settings will not be saved or shared with other applications.
(BLDevCube-ubuntu:54646): GLib-GIO-ERROR **: Settings schema 'org.gnome.settings-daemon.plugins.xsettings' does not contain a key named 'antialiasing'
Trace/breakpoint trap (core dumped)
  
So, UART programming then... fine, we'll just use one of those little CH340G dongles. I got one off Amazon, but it would not work... every time the handshake would fail in Dev Cube (hurdle 3).
Luckily, some people found out that you can program it with an RP2040 device, like the rPi Pico. I had a KB2040 from Adafruit lying around, so used that.
Which puts us at hurdle 4: How to turn a KB2040 into a serial bridge between PC and Ox64? Hook up GPIO-4 of the RP2040 to GPIO-15 of the Ox64. Hook up GPIO-5 of the RP2040 to GPIO-14 of the Ox64. Also, connect the grounds, of course. See below how to hook up for flashing the M0.\
Then we need to load our RP2040 with picoprobe which we can do by holding BOOT while powering up, and feeding it the uf2 file with picoprobe.
Ok, so now we can flash it then? Unfortunately, no, hurdle 5: DevCube is still not able to flash the Ox64, but we can use bflb-mcu-tool instead.
Ok, but with bflb-mcu-tool, we are all set! Are we? Introducing hurdle 6: we need to modify the tool before it will work for us. This means stripping DTR and RTS stuff from the tool by editing the ~/.local/lib/python3.10/site-packages/bflb_mcu_tool/libs/bflb_interface_uart.py file. Here is my copy.
  So with the Ox64 powered via MicroUSB, and the RP2040 connected to my PC using USB-C, I can finally flash something.
  For that, I took the low_load_bl808_m0.bin from the bl808 linux guide and flashed it with:
  
    
$ bflb-mcu-tool --chipname=bl808 --port=/dev/ttyACM2 --baudrate=2000000 --firmware=out/low_load_bl808_m0.bin
    
  
  After which I now get output from my Ox64, via my KB2040 to my PC:
  
    
dynamic memory init success,heap size = 156 Kbyte 
E907 start...
mtimer clk:1000000
psram clk init ok!
    
  
Ok, a modest start, but the Ox64 is talking to my PC now! Yay! Come back to my blog at a later time to see if I will manage to run Linux on the Ox64! Because as I understand it, I also need to flash something to the D0 and then flash the actual OS as well.
UPDATE: Here is PART 2.

The $8 Linux Computer (2022)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
14:00:20 UTC
23:00:20 KST

Yes, you read that right... for $8 you can run linux on a RISC-V computer. And not just a stripped down kernel without an MMU. No, the Ox64 comes with capable RISC-V cores. Heck, and not just one core, but three cores (all different.) Exciting times!
Caveat Emptor! At the time of writing, ethernet, wifi and SD-Card are not working. It's good to realize this before jumping into this.
It looks like a microcontroller, but behaves like a UNIX machine. Sweet, or what? Getting it up and running, though, is far from trivial. There are so many hurdles to take!

Zelda is back and better than ever
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
13:49:01 UTC
22:49:01 KST

Hi, friends! Welcome to Installer No. 151, your guide to the best and Verge-iest stuff in the world. (If you’re new here, welcome, get psyched because it’s officially Bad Holiday Movie Season, and also you can read all the old editions at the Installer homepage.)
Zelda is back and better than ever
Plus, in this week’s Installer: Godzilla is back too, a couple of new phone accessories, and the best pen in the world.
This week, I’ve been reading about Andrew Garfield and GTA VI and sexy joke thieves, finally actually watching The Sopranosfor the first time (I know, I know), mourning the end of Salon, reinstalling the new and extremely improved Notion Calendar, testing the new Keurig Alta coffee machine, reviewing the iPad Mini and the touchscreen MacBook Pro, and really thinking about just throwing it all away and buying an Enough Phone.
I also have for you the long-awaited return of a classic Zelda game, a monster movie worth the trek to the theater, a couple of great new phone accessories, awesome retro headphones, and more. Let’s do it.
(As always, the best part of Installer is your ideas and tips. What are you watching / reading / playing / listening to / hacking onto an E Ink display this week? Tell me everything: installer@theverge.com. And if you know someone else who might enjoy Installer, forward it to them and tell them to subscribe here.)
The Drop
- The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. I have a confession to make: I’ve never played this game all the way through. Not sure how! Just missed it. But seeing my friends and coworkers fall back in love with Hyrule has convinced me to get all the way into the remake. I genuinely can’t wait.
- Godzilla Minus Zero. Three years ago, Godzilla Minus One was a big hit and a surprisingly great movie. The sequel came with much higher expectations, but everyone seems to agree it’s a deserving follow-up. I’m not generally one for monster movies, but this series really does it for me.
- Affinity for iPad. Canva’s app now unifies all its tools in one place, just like on the Mac. Affinity makes perfect sense on the iPad, and comes with lots of Apple Pencil integration and clever touch-friendly tools. I’m no designer, but I am excited to play with this as a photo editing tool.
- The Moft Dynamic Folio Case. Here’s one for all you iPhone Duo buyers. Moft’s cases are a little complicated to figure out, but really cleverly designed to make your phone even more useful. I’m guessing this is going to be a lot better than Apple’s weird case / kickstand thing.
- The Peak Design Kickstand Travel Battery. While we’re on the subject of kickstands, here’s the one I’m buying this week. Peak Design makes good stuff, and I frequently find that I need extra juice at exactly the moment I want to watch something on a plane or train. This one’s going right into my backpack and probably never coming out.
- The Bambu Lab H3C. I am dangerously close to becoming a 3D Printer Dude. At $2,500, this one’s a little steep for my wallet, but I think my colleague Sean Hollister is right that this is what the future of 3D printing looks like. Simpler, more foolproof, easy enough even for a goofus like me to make some pretty cool stuff.
- “Ranking the world’s most popular pens for journaling.” I know we all love a good pen — and Peter McKinnon does an excellent job of breaking down a few dozen supposedly good pens to see which ones really hold up. I bought more than one pen after watching this.
- Instagram Edits for Mac. Oh, great, just as I was finally getting good at CapCut! I like the Edits mobile app a lot, and the desktop app seems to be even a little more powerful. Plus: free. This is how I finally take down Cristiano Ronaldo.
- The Sony MDR-3R headphones. Respect to Sony for taking the retro headphone trend all the way to its logical conclusion and just releasing the headphones from the original Walkman. You’ll look a little like Nathan Fielder in You Can See Everything when you wear them, and $60 ain’t cheap, but there’s something so incredibly ’70s-era cool about them.
- The Motorola Signature 27. This is just a pure tour de force of a smartphone spec sheet. Monster camera; brightest screen; giant battery. Also very cool to see a flagship phone with support for GrapheneOS, for the less Google-inclined among us. It’s expensive, and only shipping… someday… but I just like that phones like this exist.
We talked last week about Readwise Books, the new app that’s a mix of Kindle, Goodreads, and Strava. I’m a longtime fan and user of Readwise Reader, so I wasn’t shocked that I liked the new app a lot, too. And I’ve heard from a bunch of you that you do, too!
I’ve talked to Tristan Homsi, one of the Readwise cofounders, a few times over the years, and have always enjoyed hearing his thoughts about reading, apps, and digital products in general. I asked him to share his homescreen to see if I might be able to get a hint about what he’s reading and the other stuff he thinks is good. (I also asked Daniel Doyon, Readwise’s other cofounder, to share, and he didn’t respond. Daniel is dead to me.)
Here’s Tristan’s homescreen, plus some info on the apps he uses and why:
The phone: iPhone 13 (!).Believe it or not, I chose to buy this relatively outdated phone last year… not because it’s my favorite form factor or anything, but because it’s closer in specs to the median device our Readwise users have and helps me build a bit more user empathy with them. I’m able to catch performance regressions and bugs in Readwise that the developers on our team with Duos and Pro Maxes and such miss.
Let me tell you though, iOS 26 itself does not work amazingly on an iPhone 13. Lots of lagging and janky animations. I am somewhat bought into the conspiracy theory that they make the old phones suck so you buy a new one… not so much intentionally, but rather by virtue of just making each successive iOS use more and more resources, doing fancier and fancier things.
The wallpaper: My lovely wife and my cat Sophie :)
The apps: Clock, Settings, Google Calendar, Camera, Google Maps, Spotify, Bay Wheels, Oura, Eight Sleep, Auto Eight, BeReal, ChatGPT, Runmeter, Strava, Readwise Highlights, Readwise Reader, Phone, Messages, Safari.
Of course, I have all three Readwise apps front and center. Highlights and Reader as app icons, and Readwise Books in widget form at the top! It’s a great reminder to jump back into your current book (I’m reading Apple in China right now… speaking of Apple!) instead of scrolling twitter (on a later page), and seeing my friends currently reading really makes me want to jump in and join them :)
I also am a huge fan of the somewhat-defunct social app BeReal. It had its moment in 2022, but quite a few of my friends are still on there! You post once a day, can see what your close friends around the world are doing, and most importantly can’t get sucked in for more than two minutes since it limits you to a random photo or two a day! I still post basically every day, and have almost five years of memories in there.
Speaking of getting sucked in, I don’t have it pictured on my home screen, but I also have Clearspace installed, which helps me stay off of all social media.
Some other apps shown on the homescreen: “auto eight” is something I vibecoded to better control my Eight Sleep mattress (lol)… Strava and Runmeter are awesome for tracking my runs… and Bay Wheels is my main mode of commute around San Francisco daily.
I also asked Tristan to share a few things he’s into right now. Here’s what he sent back:
- I follow some amazing niche internet blogs, which you should check out if you haven’t. I love the blog Slime Mold Time Mold, famous for their citizen science and potato-maxxing. I also love Gwern.net, Deepfates, and Dynomight… among many others!
- And of course I’m always reading books. The most recent fiction series I loved was the Red Knight series — which I can only recommend if you are into dense, dark, fantasy — but man, if you are, the author is a genius.
- For non-fiction, it’s hard for me to plug anything but my favorite book of all time, The Beginning of Infinity. It’s not the easiest to get through, but if you do it will likely change the way you see the world, as it did for me!

But as of this week, you can now at least try it for yourself, since Kagi will give you 50 free searches before even asking you to sign up. You get 100 more when you do, even before you pay.
If you search a lot, and miss what Google used to be, give Kagi a whirl. I am a very happy customer, and suspect I will be for a long time.
